Best Treatment Sequence to Obtain the Desired Result

7

Orthodontic treatment time
Fig 3-25 A schematic presentation of the timing of orthognathic surgery for patients with dentofacial deformities. The
blue line represents the orthodontic treatment time. (1) Surgery is performed for patients not requiring any orthodontic
alignment of their dentition. (2) Surgery first is performed before any orthodontic tooth alignment, and all the orthodontic
tooth movements will take place after surgical correction of the jaw relationship. (3) Orthodontic banding occurs in the
beginning of treatment. (4) Surgery is performed early in the orthodontic phase. (5) Surgery is performed once the dentition has been orthodontically prepared to enhance surgical accuracy and the occlusion. (6) The orthodontic appliances
are removed and the retention phase starts. (7) In some cases, surgery is postponed indefinitely for various reasons (eg,
medical, financial, psychologic).

Surgery without orthodontics
The indications to perform orthognathic surgery without
orthodontic treatment are limited. The following groups
of patients may be candidates for surgical intervention
without presurgical orthodontic preparation:
1. Patient with severe obstructive sleep apnea and a Class I
occlusion who require maxillomandibular advancement
2. Adolescent patients with TMJ ankylosis
3. Patients with certain syndromes (eg, hemifacial microsomia)

Surgery first
This approach involves performing the orthognathic
surgery first with the specific intention to complete the
treatment by orthodontically aligning the dentition in
a second phase. Indications for surgery first are limited,
and preliminary treatment planning is very demanding,
requiring experienced clinicians.
Hernández-Alfaro and Guijarro-Martı'nez (2014)
suggest that performing orthognathic surgery first should
be considered (1) in cases with a skeletal malocclusion if no
tooth extractions are required or (2) if the patient’s motivation for treatment is esthetic improvement or to resolve

a sleep disorder. They also state that the treatment should
be managed by an experienced orthodontist. Exclusion
factors for surgery first include active periodontal disease,
active TMJ disorders (TMDs), severe 3D facial asymmetry,
Class II deep bite malocclusions, and transverse maxillary deficiency requiring surgically assisted rapid palatal
expansion (SARPE).

Surgery early in the orthodontic phase
In some instances, it would be advantageous to perform
surgery early in the orthodontic phase of treatment and
to facilitate and enhance the orthodontic treatment. Some
examples include the following:
1. Patients with a scissors crossbite
2. Patients with a Class II deep bite
3. Alveolar cleft patients
4. Patients with severe crowding
5. Patients with certain syndromes (eg, hemifacial microsomia)
6. Patients requiring minimal orthodontic preparation
and exhibiting an occlusion that would be acceptable
following surgery soon after banding

83

3 | Diagnosis and Treatment Planning

Surgery later in the orthodontic phase
Some patients require tooth movements to allow for surgical
jaw repositioning to be accurate and the planned occlusion to
be achieved. In cases like this, surgery is performed later in
the orthodontic treatment phase. It is usually preferable to
perform basic restorative dental and periodontal treatment
before beginning the preoperative orthodontic phase. This
sequence will ensure that the dentition and supporting dental
tissues are sound. It is also preferable to remove any impacted
teeth at this stage. It is advantageous to achieve stable interdigitation of the teeth at the time of surgery for two reasons:
(1) A good occlusion enhances postoperative stability and
(2) it allows the orthodontist to finalize the occlusion in a
relatively short time. The patient’s enthusiasm for continued
orthodontic treatment is likely to dwindle after surgery.
In general, the surgery should not be performed too
early in the orthodontic phase for the following reasons:
• Delaying surgery allows the surgeon to position the
jaws more accurately.
• Stability will improve because better interdigitation
of the teeth can be achieved if the dental arches are
better prepared.
• After surgery, the patient’s appearance improves; as a
consequence, motivation for correction of small orthodontic discrepancies diminishes and cooperation falters.
Surgery performed before presurgical orthodontic goals
have been achieved will often lead to compromised surgical
treatment, increased postoperative orthodontic treatment
time, questionable stability, and unfavorable esthetics.
Most orthodontic “mistakes” can be solved with orthodontic means, but surgical “mistakes” can and should be
corrected by surgery (ie, reoperation).
In most cases, orthognathic surgery is performed once
the planned presurgical orthodontic dental alignment has
been completed. This will allow the surgeon to achieve the
planned occlusion and usually only requires a relatively
short period of postoperative orthodontic refinement. It is
advantageous not to extend the postoperative orthodontic treatment beyond 3 to 6 months. If the postoperative
orthodontic phase takes longer than anticipated, the orthodontist should reassess the case, possibly with new records,
to determine the reason for the prolonged postoperative
orthodontic treatment. Possible reasons may include poor
patient cooperation, inaccurate surgery requiring longer
orthodontic correction, skeletal relapse, and/or orthodontic
relapse. The orthodontist should not attempt to correct

84

significant skeletal relapse with orthodontics, but rather
refer the patient back to the surgeon for reassessment.
In determining placement of dental implants, overall
functional and esthetic results should be carefully considered. Implants can be placed during orthognathic surgery,
saving the patient a second surgical procedure. Keep in
mind that placement of dental implants during orthognathic
surgery will limit any orthodontic tooth movement after
surgery. However, the ideal time for placement of implants
is after an orthodontic retention period of approximately
3 months. If bone grafting is required for the implants, the
grafts can be placed during the orthognathic surgery. Prosthodontic restoration of dental implants should begin as soon
as is practical. Early restoration will improve orthodontic
stability and reduce the possibility of prepared spaces closing, should patients not cooperate in wearing retainers.

Development of Visual
Treatment Objectives
During the last two decades, technological advances have
had a major impact on the planning and efficiency of orthognathic surgery. Virtual surgical planning has become an
increasingly important tool, especially for two-jaw surgery,
correction of facial asymmetry, TMJ replacement, treatment
of syndromic cases, and more. However, it is still necessary
to be able to (1) develop an orthodontic prediction of tooth
movement and (2) use cephalometric tracing to surgically
reposition the jaws. Understanding these basic concepts is
an important step in visualizing the orthognathic treatment.
Accurate and realistic visual treatment objectives are
developed from the lateral cephalometric radiograph tracing in combination with all the data obtained from the
systematic patient evaluation. There are two types of visual
treatment objectives: the pretreatment visual treatment
objective and the immediate presurgical prediction tracing.
The pretreatment visual treatment objective is developed once the primary surgical procedure has been selected.
The following questions are used in its development:
• Will the selected surgical procedure produce the
required facial esthetic changes?
• Will it be physically possible to create the skeletal
change within the biologic boundaries?
• Will it be possible to orthodontically produce the tooth
movements called for by the proposed surgery?

Orthodontic Prediction Tracing

The pretreatment visual treatment objective is used for
overall treatment planning. It consists of (1) an orthodontic prediction tracing illustrating the desired presurgical
orthodontic tooth movement and the resulting soft tissue
changes and (2) a surgical prediction tracing predicting
the surgical repositioning of the jaws and subsequent soft
tissue changes. The magnitude of the surgical movements
of the jaws is not important at this stage as long as the
procedure falls within the boundaries of good surgical
technique. If more than one surgical or orthodontic option
is under consideration, a series of prediction tracings
should be made and all advantages and disadvantages
of each treatment possibility analyzed. Where possible,
simple treatment is best.
The immediate presurgical prediction tracing, which
is created a few days before surgery, plans the definitive
surgical movements and predicts the soft tissue changes.
The presurgical orthodontic treatment is complete, and
accurate tracing and measurement of surgical movements
is now paramount.
The visual treatment objective has several advantages:
• It can accurately predict the soft tissue profile that will
result after the proposed orthodontic tooth movements
and surgical skeletal repositioning. The predicted soft
tissue profile can be assessed and the treatment plan
adjusted, if necessary.
• It enables the orthodontist and surgeon to investigate
treatment options and evaluate the advantages and
disadvantages of each option before treatment starts.
• It helps analyze the need for tooth extractions and
indicates which teeth to extract.
• It helps assess the need for adjunctive surgical procedures (eg, genioplasty).
• It monitors the progress of orthodontic treatment
using the orthodontic prediction.
• It helps assess the postsurgical skeletal movements.
• It acts as a communication medium between the orthodontist and the surgeon, as well as between the clinician
and the patient.

Orthodontic Prediction Tracing
The presurgical position of the teeth dictates the surgical
movement of the jaws and, ultimately, the soft tissue facial
balance. Correct planning of orthodontic tooth positioning
before surgery and accurate execution of the presurgical
orthodontic plan will enhance the surgical potential and
therefore the esthetic result. Because of the myriad treatment possibilities, it is impossible to discuss method and
development for every conceivable treatment objective.
Thus, the following sections demonstrate the development
of a few prediction tracings that will be helpful in most
treatment planning.

Mandibular advancement
Figure 3-26 shows the pretreatment cephalometric analysis
of a patient who will benefit from a mandibular advancement. Cephalometric parameters, discussed in chapter 2,
may be used as indicators of the dental, soft tissue, and
hard tissue objectives. As has been noted, the cephalometric values should be used as a guide, and compromise
(dental, skeletal, or soft tissue) may be necessary in formulating the best possible treatment for each patient.
When mandibular advancement is contemplated, the
shape of the chin should be assessed as part of the orthodontic treatment planning. Esthetic planning of the soft
tissue of the chin should assess the horizontal position of
the soft tissue pogonion (Pog′), but the actual shape of
the chin is more important in the overall esthetics. The
following factors should be considered in the assessment
of chin esthetics (Fig 3-27): vertical height of the chin,
lower lip length, amount of lower lip vermilion exposed,
depth of the labiomental fold, lower lip–chin–throat angle,
chin-throat length, curvature of the chin, and horizontal
relation of the labrale inferior (Li) and Pog′.
Figure 3-28 illustrates chin shape in three patients with
ideal incisal relationships and Pog′ in the same horizontal
position. Because of the variation in the shapes of their
chins, however, the three patients have different esthetic
appearances.
Therefore, when there is a clear indication for genioplasty to improve the shape of the chin, it should be
incorporated in the orthodontic prediction tracing so the
presurgical orthodontic tooth positioning will allow for
the correct amount of mandibular advancement and incorporate the planned amount of chin advancement for the
best esthetic result.

Fig 3-27 Parameters used in the evaluation of the overall esthetics of the chin.
The esthetic line (E-line) (1) is drawn from nasal tip (Pn) to Pog′. The height of
the chin (2) should be equivalent to two-thirds of the lower third of the face
(subnasale-stomion:stomion–soft tissue menton = 1:2). Also known as lower
lip length, chin height should be 40 ± 2 mm (females) and 44 ± 2 mm (males).
Vermilion exposure (3): The lower lip exhibits approximately 25% more vermilion than the upper lip. Depth of the labiomental fold (4): The labiomental
fold should have a smooth, concave curve connecting the lower lip with
the chin. Mandibular-prognathic cases often exhibit flat labiomental folds,
whereas mandibular-deficient cases often have deep folds. Lower lip–chin–
throat angle (5): 110 ± 8 degrees. This angle tends to be smaller than normal
in mandibular prognathism and larger than normal in mandibular deficiency
cases. Chin-throat length (6): normal, 42 ± 6 mm. An advancement genioplasty
will clearly increase this distance. The distance will decrease when the chin is
set back. Curvature of the chin (7): The shape of the chin is enhanced if the
profile forms a smooth S shape from stomion inferius to soft tissue menton.
The lower lip should be 2 ± 2 mm behind the E-line (8).

Fig 3-29 (a) Step 1: The original tracing. (b) Step 2: The prediction tracing. (c) Prediction of bony advancement of the chin (ratio of soft tissue to hard
tissue advancement = 0.9:1.0) is done after the prediction tracing is moved to the left until predicted soft tissue at Pog′ coincides with that of the original
tracing (arrow).

Step 1
Create the original tracing by tracing the hard and soft tissue,
without analysis lines, on a separate sheet of acetate. In this
case, the facial depth angle and facial contour angle are used
as indicators of the “ideal” hard and soft tissue positions,
respectively. The facial depth angle is formed by passing
a line from nasion (N) through A-point and the Frankfort
horizontal (FH) plane (with the maxilla in good position:
males, 90 degrees; females, 89 degrees). An extension of
the N-A line should go through Pog′. The facial contour
angle is formed between the upper facial plane (UFP) and
lower facial plane (LFP) and should be –11 to –15 degrees
for males and –13 to –17 degrees for females. Also, draw a
vertical line on the mandible posterior to the second molar,
representing the vertical osteotomy line of the sagittal split
osteotomy; draw a horizontal line on the chin, representing
the genioplasty osteotomy line (Fig 3-29a).
Step 2
In this case, clinical and cephalometric assessment of the
chin shape indicates an advancement genioplasty. Begin

the prediction tracing by tracing the mandible anterior
to the vertical osteotomy line and above the horizontal
osteotomy line in red on a clean piece of acetate. The teeth,
lower lip, and chin are traced in dotted lines. Draw the ideal
soft tissue chin shape on this tracing (Fig 3-29b). Bearing in
mind that soft tissue change in relation to hard tissue repositioning of the chin is 0.9:1.0, draw in the actual required
bony advancement by sliding the prediction tracing to the
left until the predicted soft tissue coincides with the soft
tissue on the original tracing. The Pog′ should now be at,
or very close to, the desired horizontal position, and the
shape of the chin should be esthetically pleasing. Trace the
occlusal plane (Fig 3-29c).
In this case, the mandibular occlusal plane is level.
However, patients with Class II malocclusion often have
deep bites with an accentuated curve of Spee. Preoperative
versus postoperative leveling of the curve of Spee should
be considered carefully because the choice affects facial
esthetics, the amount of surgical advancement, and the
need for genioplasty. An excessive curve of Spee should
be leveled before surgery in the following cases:

Fig 3-29 (cont) (d) The curve of Spee is not leveled, and as the mandible is advanced, the mandible will rotate clockwise. The incisors will advance (2) more
than the Pog′ (3), whereas correction of the deep bite (1) will increase the vertical height of the chin (4) by the same amount. (e) Three occlusal planes: the
molar-incisor plane of the mandible (A), the functional occlusal plane (B), and the molar-incisor plane of the maxilla (C). (f) Step 3.

• When the chin needs to be advanced the same distance
as the mandibular incisors
• When little vertical increase in the lower facial height
is indicated; in high-angle cases, the vertical increase
is slightly more than in low-angle cases because of the
steepness of the occlusal plane
• When less decompensation of the incisors will be
necessary to achieve the desired chin projection
The curve of Spee should not be leveled prior to surgery
when a clockwise rotation of the distal portion of the
mandible will be advantageous (Fig 3-29d); for example:
• When the incisors need to be advanced more than the
chin
• When the anterior vertical height of the lower third
needs to be increased by the amount of excess of the
overbite
• When a deep labiomental fold (usually present with deep
bites) will improve because of the advancement of the
mandibular incisors and downward rotation of the chin
Taking these factors into account, the clinician should
choose the plane of advancement. The mandible can be
advanced along one of three occlusal planes (Fig 3-29e): (1)
the functional occlusal plane, (2) the molar-incisor plane of
the maxilla, or (3) the molar-incisor plane of the mandible
(in this case, all three planes are the same).

88

Step 3
Slide the prediction tracing to the right along the chosen
occlusal plane (in this case, the functional occlusal plane)
until the Pog coincides with the facial depth line and the
Pog′ coincides with the LFP line (or best fit; Fig 3-29f ).
The soft tissue should take precedence because it will
determine the esthetic result.
Step 4
Trace the rest of the facial structures. The maxillary teeth
are drawn in dotted lines (Fig 3-29g). It is now clear that
the mandibular advancement has resulted in an anterior
crossbite. Can the teeth be orthodontically positioned in
their ideal relationship to bone and to each other in the
new jaw relationship? The clinician has various options:
• Retraction of the mandibular incisors with or without
extractions
• Advancement of the maxillary incisors
• Less advancement of the mandible and more advancement of the chin

Step 5
Various cephalometric parameters may now be used on the
prediction tracing to place the teeth in their ideal position in each
arch and in relation to each other. Draw the incisors in their best
possible position on the prediction tracing (Fig 3-29h).

Orthodontic Prediction Tracing

g

h

i

Fig 3-29 (cont) (g) Step 4. (h) Step 5. (i) Step 7.

Step 6
The predicted incisor positions are now carefully studied. In this case, the mandibular incisors will have to be
retracted. However, the clinician should keep in mind
not to excessively retract the maxillary incisors, because
this would undermine lip support and limit mandibular
advancement. The maxillary arch in Class II cases often
needs some expansion to accommodate the mandibular
arch after advancement. This expansion, in turn, often
offers sufficient space for ideal positioning of the maxillary incisors. Extraction of premolars (first or second)
may be indicated where crowding and an occlusal curve
are present. Every 1 mm of arch leveling will increase the
anteroposterior arch length by 1 mm. However, there will
be an approximate 1-mm increase in arch length for every
1 mm of expansion of the arch at the canine area and a
4-mm increase for a 1-mm arch expansion in the molar
area. The clinician can make a decision regarding tooth
extraction by taking the following factors into account:
• Current incisor position
• Required incisor position to facilitate the correct
amount of mandibular advancement
• Amount and area of crowding
• Extent of the occlusal curve
• Need for arch expansion
• Possibility of interdental stripping

Step 7
The required arch length can now be established. If
tooth extractions are indicated, the width of the teeth
to be extracted should be subtracted from the existing arch length. The remainder of space (if any) can be
closed by advancing the molars. The feasibility of the
predicted position of the incisors will now be evident.
In some cases, it may be necessary to make small dental
compromises because of lack of arch length (even with
tooth extractions), thin mandibular symphysis limiting
tooth movement, or increase in treatment time. In this
case, two premolars should be extracted to achieve the
amount of mandibular incisor retraction indicated on the
prediction tracing. The remaining space will be made up
by advancing the molars.
The molar relationship can also be established. In this
case, where extractions are not indicated in the maxillary
arch, the maxillary molar does not move and is placed
in a Class III relationship to the mandibular molar. The
mandibular molar is advanced slightly to close the remaining extraction space once the incisor has been retracted
sufficiently (Fig 3-29i).

89

3 | Diagnosis and Treatment Planning

j

k

Step 8
The soft tissue profile prediction can now be completed.
The upper lip will change very little because of the small
amount of maxillary incisor retraction. In contrast, the
lower lip will roll back subsequent to retraction (decompensation) of the mandibular incisors, as well as because
of the elimination of the effect of the maxillary incisors
on the lower lip. The rotation of the lower lip affects the
soft tissue superior to the labiomental fold. Generally, the
lower lip has the same thickness as the upper lip (Fig 3-29j).
Figure 3-29k illustrates the completed prediction
tracing (visual treatment objective stage). The tracing
indicates the intended orthodontic tooth movement, the
surgical repositioning of the mandible and chin, and the
expected soft tissue result.

Maxillary advancement
Figure 3-30a illustrates a cephalometric tracing and analysis
of a patient who will benefit from a maxillary advancement
procedure. Begin the original tracing by tracing all the relevant hard and soft tissues on a clean piece of acetate. Also
trace all the teeth because this will assist in the prediction
of the orthodontic tooth movement. Draw the Le Fort I
osteotomy line on the maxilla as well as the mandibular
occlusal plane. The following lines are drawn and angles
constructed to assist in the ideal positioning of the teeth
and in the surgical repositioning of the maxilla (Fig 3-30b):
• The facial depth line is drawn by dropping a line perpendicular to FH from N. The line should be tangent to
A-point, as well as to Pog. In this case, however, the line
is 5 mm ahead of the A-point, confirming the diagnosis
of maxillary anteroposterior deficiency.

90

Fig 3-29 (cont) (j) Step 8. (k) Completed prediction tracing.

• The ideal position of subnasale (Sn) is predicted by
constructing the ideal facial contour angle for the
patient. In this case, a facial contour angle of –13
degrees is chosen. The UFP and the LFP are drawn at
an angle of 13 degrees with the help of a protractor. The
glabella (G) and Pog′ are static; Sn is advanced.

Step 1
Trace all the facial structures that will not be affected by
the surgery on a clean piece of acetate. Trace the cranial
base, soft tissue chin below the labiomental fold, hard tissue
above the Le Fort I osteotomy line, mandible, mandibular
occlusal plane, forehead, and soft tissue of the nose above
the supratip break. The mandibular teeth (molar and incisor) are drawn in a dotted line. Trace the facial depth line
and LFP in the midface region. The facial depth line is an
indicator of the required A-point position on the maxilla,
and the LFP indicates the required position of Sn. This
tracing will develop into the prediction tracing (Fig 3-30c).
Step 2
Slide the prediction tracing to the left. The original tracing
will move to the right, advancing the maxilla. Advance the
maxilla on the original tracing until A-point is tangent to
the facial depth line and Sn is tangent to LFP or best fit. In
this case, A-point is moved just past the facial depth line
whereas Sn falls short of LFP. Place the maxillary incisor
tip on the horizontal incisor line (Fig 3-30d). Now draw
the maxillary molars and incisors with dotted lines on the
prediction tracing (Fig 3-30e).
The prediction tracing can now be carefully studied
and the following observations made:

An increased incisor overjet is present.
The maxilla is advanced by 6.5 mm.
The maxilla is inferiorly repositioned by 2 mm.
The molars are now in a Class II relationship.
The clinician has the following options:

• Advance the maxilla less to get a better occlusal relationship, and retract the maxillary incisors. However,
there is still crowding in the maxillary arch. A small
advancement would compromise the esthetic result.
• Extract the maxillary first premolars and retract the
maxillary incisors, improving their angulation. The
remaining space will be taken up by advancement of
the molars. Decompensate the mandibular incisors.
Keep the maxillary advancement at 6.5 mm.
It is clear that the second treatment option will achieve
the better result. Trace the maxillary hard tissue and the Le
Fort I osteotomy line (Fig 3-30f). Figure 3-30g illustrates
what the prediction tracing should look like at this stage.
Step 3
Draw the maxillary incisor on the prediction tracing with
the aid of cephalometric guidelines, keeping the tooth in
the central trough of bone. Draw in the maxillary molar
by advancing it slightly (the first premolars have been
extracted), closing the remaining space. Decompensate

the mandibular incisor by moving it slightly labially; then
draw the tooth on the prediction tracing. The mandibular
molar position will change very little (Fig 3-30h).
Step 4
The prediction tracing is now superimposed on its position
on the original tracing. The surgical change in the position of
the maxilla is clear (Fig 3-30i). The soft tissue changes subsequent to the repositioning of the maxilla can now be drawn in:
• Vermilion (stomion superius): 50% to 75% of horizontal
movement
• Nasolabial angle: decreases 1 to 4 degrees per 1-mm
advancement
• Nasal tip: advances 30% (variable) and can be controlled
First, draw the predicted soft tissue change with a
dotted line (Fig 3-30j). When you are satisfied with the
prediction, complete the line. Draw the soft tissue of the
lower lip above the labiomental fold. Because of the effect
of the upper lip on the lower lip, slight curling of the lower
lip is expected (see Fig 3-30j).
Step 5
Complete the visual treatment objective by measuring the
horizontal and vertical change between the two Le Fort I
maxillary osteotomy lines, and note the orthodontic tooth
movements (Fig 3-30k).

Superior repositioning of the maxilla,
advancement of the mandible, and
advancement genioplasty
Figure 3-31a shows the pretreatment cephalometric tracing
of a patient with a Class II malocclusion, vertical maxillary
excess, mandibular anteroposterior deficiency, and microgenia. The clinician must ensure that the teeth are in centric
relation and the lips are in repose during radiography.
Start the prediction by tracing the hard and soft tissues on
clean acetate, deleting the analysis lines (the original tracing).
To assist in developing the prediction, construct the following
lines and angles on the original tracing (Fig 3-31b):
• The facial depth line is drawn through N and A-point
and should be perpendicular to the FH plane. In this
case, the line is constructed through N perpendicular
to FH, and it is clear that A-point is slightly ahead of
the line. This line is an indicator of the final position
of the bony chin (Pog). Therefore, plan for Pog also to
be slightly ahead of this line.
• The facial contour angle is formed by UFP and LFP
and should be –11 to –15 degrees in males and –13 to
–17 degrees in females. In this case, a –15-degree facial
contour angle is chosen and constructed to show the
clinician where the soft tissue chin (Pog′) should be.

• This patient will benefit from a Le Fort I maxillary osteotomy (superior repositioning), a bilateral sagittal split
mandibular osteotomy (advancement), and genioplasty
(advancement). For accuracy of measurement later,
draw the osteotomy lines as close as possible to the
areas where the osteotomies will be performed.
Step 1
On a clean piece of acetate, trace all the facial structures
that will not be affected by the treatment: the cranial base,
soft tissue of the forehead, and soft tissue of the nose
above the supratip break. This tracing will develop into the
prediction tracing. The ideal vertical position of the maxillary incisors is indicated by a horizontal line approximately
3 mm inferior to the upper lip. This decision is based on
the required amount of incisor exposure under the upper
lip after maxillary superior repositioning, as discussed in
chapter 2. Bear in mind that there will be some lip shortening. A vertical line indicating the ideal anteroposterior
position of the labial surface of the maxillary incisor is
drawn crossing the horizontal line. (The decision of where
to draw these lines is based on the soft tissue requirements
of each case.) Trace the inferior part of the constructed
LFP (Fig 3-31c).

Step 2
Apply finger pressure just posterior to the condylion on the
prediction tracing, and rotate the tracing counterclockwise
around the condylion. In this case, the patient has a deep
bite and an accentuated curve of Spee that will need to be
leveled before surgery. Therefore, the maxillary occlusal
plane will be the occlusal plane of choice.

to the horizontal line (Fig 3-31d). Draw the mandibular
ramus proximal to the vertical osteotomy line as well as
the maxillary occlusal plane (Fig 3-31e).

Slide the prediction tracing to the left along the occlusal
plane until the soft tissue of the chin (Pog′) is tangent to the
predicted LFP. After careful study of the distal section of the
mandible on the original tracing in relation to the prediction
tracing, three observations can be made (Fig 3-31f):
1. The mandibular incisor is ahead of the vertical line, indicating the desired labial surface of the maxillary incisor.
2. The mandibular incisor edge is 3 mm above the horizontal line, indicating the vertical position of the maxillary
incisor edge.
3. The vertical osteotomy line is 8 mm ahead of the osteotomy line on the original tracing, indicating that an
8-mm surgical advancement is required to achieve the
predicted chin position.

94

The clinician has the following options:
• Retraction of the mandibular incisors. In this case, the
angle of the mandibular incisors is acceptable. Any
retraction will make the teeth very upright and require
teeth to be extracted.
• Leveling the curve of Spee. This option will increase
the mandibular arch length and thus necessitate slight
advancement of the mandibular incisors. Extraction of
teeth may be indicated.
• Less advancement of the mandible. This option will
compromise the esthetics.
• Less advancement of the mandible combined with an
advancement genioplasty. The patient has a small chin
and a large, everted lower lip. An advancement of the
chin will benefit the patient.
From these considerations, it is clear that the best
option is to level the mandibular arch without tooth
extractions. The mandibular incisors will move slightly
forward. Advance the mandible less, and combine the
mandibular surgery with an advancement genioplasty.
Steps 4 through 9 are taken to test this decision.

osteotomy. Do not trace the mandible inferior to the genio-

Final Surgical Visual Treatment Prediction

Fig 3-31 (cont) (p) Completed visual treatment objective.

Final Surgical Visual Treatment
Prediction
The surgical visual treatment objective is based on the
immediate preoperative cephalometric radiograph. The
preoperative orthodontic preparation is now completed,
and the immediate preoperative radiograph will accurately
determine the skeletal surgical movements necessary to
achieve the functional and esthetic treatment goals. The
visual treatment objective can also be used after surgery
to check whether the predicted surgical results have been
achieved.
Because of the extreme diversity of surgical procedures
and the combination of procedures used in the surgical
correction of dentofacial deformities, it is impossible to
illustrate the method of developing the cephalometric
prediction tracing for every conceivable deformity. By
creating the visual treatment predictions through trial
and error and comparing the actual soft and hard tissue
treatment results with the predicted results, surgeons will
develop their own data for soft tissue predictions to perfect
their prediction tracing techniques.

A few days before surgery, a cephalometric radiograph is
obtained with the patient’s teeth in centric relation and the
lips in repose. To create the original tracing, the radiograph
is accurately traced to demonstrate all the relevant hard
and soft tissue structures. All the dental structures are also
traced to accurately demonstrate the crowns and roots of
the teeth. The analysis lines are not drawn on this tracing.
The tracing is removed from the radiograph for better visualization when constructing the prediction tracing.

Advancement genioplasty
Step 1
The patient shown in the original tracing in Fig 3-32a will
benefit from an advancement genioplasty. Draw a line on
the original tracing representing the osteotomy cut on the
chin. Bear in mind anatomical structures, such as the mental
foramen and roots of the teeth. Also remember that the
angle at which the osteotomy is performed will influence the
vertical dimension of the chin as it is advanced (see chapter
5). Draw the facial contour angle formed by the upper and
lower facial planes. Construct a predicted lower facial plane
(in this case, an angle of –15 degrees to the upper facial plane
is chosen) using dotted lines (Fig 3-32b).

Step 2
Place a clean sheet of acetate over the original tracing.
Trace the osteotomy line and all skeletal and soft tissue
structures that will remain unchanged by the surgical
procedure—in this case, everything above the genioplasty
line. This tracing is called the prediction tracing (Fig 3-32c).
Step 3
The clinician, guided by all the available references for the
ideal soft tissue position of the chin (in this case, the facial
contour angle is used) and an artistic sensibility, draws the
soft tissue of the chin on the prediction tracing (Fig 3-32d).
Step 4
The prediction tracing is now moved toward the left until
soft tissue chin line of the original tracing is tangent to,
or just ahead of, soft tissue chin line of the prediction
tracing (soft tissue change to hard tissue movement is
90% to 100%). Trace the skeletal change as superimposed
on the original tracing (Fig 3-32e). Vertical changes can

98

now be made where indicated (eg, ostectomy to vertically
shorten the chin, downgrafting for vertical increase, or
changing the angulation of the osteotomy so that the chin
will slide superiorly when advanced or slide more horizontally when reduced). The skeletal advancement can
be measured and recorded on the completed prediction
tracing (Fig 3-32f ).

the vertical cut of the bilateral sagittal split osteotomy. This
tracing is called the original tracing (Fig 3-33a).

trace all skeletal and soft tissues that will not be affected by

of the maxillary incisor on the lower lip is now removed,

and the lower lip will curl back, reducing the vermilion
exposure. Figure 3-33f shows the completed prediction.
The amount of mandibular advancement can be determined by measuring the distance between the two vertical
osteotomy lines.

Mandibular setback
Step 1
Draw a line on the original tracing to represent the vertical
cut of the bilateral sagittal split osteotomy on the body
of the mandible. This line should be drawn as close as
possible to where the actual osteotomy will be performed
(Fig 3-34a).
Step 2
Place a clean sheet of acetate over the original tracing, and
trace all skeletal and soft tissues that will not be affected by
the surgery. Do not trace the upper lip or the hard and soft
tissues distal to the mandibular osteotomy line (Fig 3-34b).

Step 3
Slide the prediction tracing to the right (the distal mandible will move to the left) until the best interincisal and
molar relationship is achieved (Fig 3-34c). Trace the
skeletal tissue as well as the osteotomy line of the distal
segment. Trace the soft tissue of the chin below the labiomental fold (soft tissue change to hard tissue movement
is 90% to 100% at the pogonion; Fig 3-34d).
Step 4
The effect of the mandibular incisor on the upper lip (Class
III deep bite cases) is now removed, and the lip will move
back slightly. Draw the new position of the upper lip. In
some Class III cases (eg, open bite cases), the mandibular
incisors will have no effect on the upper lip. When this is
true, as in this case, the lower lip can be traced in from the
start. Thus, with the prediction tracing still in the same
position, draw the lower lip. In Class III deep bite cases
(overclosure), the lower lip will change in response to
the influence of the upper lip and maxillary incisor. The
amount of mandibular setback can now be determined on
the completed prediction tracing by measuring the distance
between the two vertical osteotomy lines (Fig 3-34e).

Maxillary advancement
Step 1
The original tracing of a patient with a Class III malocclusion is illustrated in Fig 3-35a. Draw a line on the original
tracing to represent the Le Fort I maxillary osteotomy.
This line should be drawn as close as possible to where
the actual osteotomy will be performed on the maxilla; it
makes the measurement of maxillary repositioning more
accurate.
The line should be parallel to the occlusal plane of the
maxilla so that the maxilla will be advanced along this
plane. If the line is angled upward, the height of the maxilla
will be reduced; if bone contact is maintained, the bite will
open anteriorly. If the line is angled inferiorly, the maxilla
will slide downward. In a Le Fort I advancement procedure, the final anteroposterior position of the maxilla is
dictated by the mandibular dentition. However, the vertical position of the maxilla is controlled by the surgeon.
The mandible will autorotate (clockwise subsequent to
an increase in the maxillary height and counterclockwise
subsequent to superior repositioning of the maxilla). The
clinician can therefore decide on the final vertical position

The nasal tip advances 30% (although this is variable) and
can be controlled.

Maxillary superior repositioning
Step 1
The original tracing of a patient requiring superior repositioning of the maxilla for correction of vertical maxillary
excess is shown in Fig 3-36a. The radiograph must show
the lips in repose and the teeth in centric occlusion. When
correcting vertical maxillary excess, the maxillary central
incisor–upper lip relationship forms the basis of the development of a visual treatment objective.

102

g

Fig 3-35 ( cont) ( e) The maxillary teeth on
the original tracing are now in occlusion with
the mandibular teeth of the prediction tracing.
(f) Step 3. (g) Soft tissue prediction of the tip.
(h) Prediction of the lower lip of the nose and
upper lip. (i) Completed prediction tracing.

Draw a horizontal line on the original tracing with a
vertical step that simulates the Le Fort I maxillary osteotomy as close as possible to where the actual osteotomy
will be performed on the maxilla. The most important
decision of the surgical treatment objective—the final
vertical position of the maxillary incisor tip—must now
be made. The decision is made with the aid of cephalometric guidelines, knowledge of soft tissue response to
maxillary superior repositioning, clinical experience, and
artistic sense. The following are a few guidelines to assist
in making this decision:
• Maxillary incisor exposure of 1 to 4 mm under the upper
lip is considered normal.
• Less maxillary incisor will show under long upper lips, and
more maxillary incisor will show under short upper lips.
• Never treat the gummy smile. The smile may expose
a larger amount of gingiva than the amount of superior repositioning indicated by the incisor exposure
under the upper lip. Treatment of the gummy smile will
lead to excessive superior repositioning of the maxilla,
resulting in a toothless appearance.
• Superior repositioning of the maxilla combined with maxillary setback undermines soft tissue support of the upper
lip and paranasal areas, leading to poor esthetic results.

• Superior repositioning of the maxilla will lead to upper
lip shortening (10% to 20% of the amount of superior

• Never treat the interlabial gap. Correction of vertical
maxillary excess and establishment of the ideal toothlip relationship will create a lip seal in most cases. An
interlabial gap of 1 to 3 mm is acceptable and often very
attractive. The amount of superior repositioning should
not be guided by the interlabial distance.
• The upper lip length, lip thickness, and overall esthetics are also influenced by the surgical technique (see
chapter 5).
• Err on the long side rather than the short side in superior repositioning of the maxilla.

Maxillary segmental superior repositioning
with extraction of first premolars and
advancement of the mandible
Step 1
Begin the prediction by tracing the original tracing (Fig
3-37a). Draw lines on the original tracing to simulate the
osteotomies. A horizontal line with a step simulating the Le
Fort I osteotomy is drawn as close as possible to the actual
osteotomy on the maxilla. Two vertical lines indicating
the limits of the interdental ostectomy are then drawn
between the second maxillary premolar and the canine.
The first premolars will be removed at the time of surgery
and the space closed surgically.
The very important decision—the predicted vertical
position of the maxillary incisor—is now made. Guided by
the information on the ideal incisor–upper lip relationship
discussed elsewhere, a horizontal line is drawn to indicate
the vertical position of the maxillary incisor. A vertical line
transecting this line, indicating the desired anteroposterior position of the maxillary incisor, is then drawn. The
final position of the maxillary incisor tip is indicated by
the intersection of these two lines. The fact that surgery
will involve both jaws allows the clinician to choose both
the final vertical and the final anteroposterior position of
the maxillary incisor. Guidelines to assist in the vertical
positioning of the incisor have been discussed earlier.

The following guidelines will help the clinician locate
the ideal horizontal positioning of the maxillary incisor:
• The maxillary incisors are responsible for upper lip
support. Excessive retraction of the anterior maxillary segment will result in loss of lip support and poor
esthetic results.
• Retraction of the anterior segment will increase the
nasolabial angle and accentuate the nose.
• Lip thickness is often a good indicator of how much
retraction of the anterior segment a patient will tolerate before the lip will react to the tooth movement.
In general, the thickness of the upper and lower lips
is equal.
• Vermilion exposure of the upper lip will decrease after
retraction of the anterior maxillary segment.
• Avoid excessive retraction of the anterior teeth. A fuller
lip is esthetically more pleasing than a flat, unsupported
upper lip.
A vertical line simulating the vertical osteotomy of the
bilateral sagittal split osteotomy is drawn on the body of
the mandible (Fig 3-37b).
Step 2
Place a clean sheet of acetate over the original tracing,
and trace all hard and soft tissue structures that will not

Fig 3-39 (a) Presurgical cephalometric analysis of the patient in case 1. (b) Surgical prediction tracing according to conventional principles. The surgery involves advancement of both the maxilla and

Rotation of the maxillomandibular complex
In all the previous prediction tracings, the final occlusal plane was dictated by the mandibular occlusal plane
(after autorotation in cases where vertical repositioning
of the maxilla occurred). The mandible (and therefore
the mandibular occlusal plane) will rotate around a point
at or just posterior to the condyle. Any anteroposterior
changes will take place along this “new” plane (Fig 3-38).
This principle is not adhered to in treatment planning
requiring rotation of the maxillomandibular complex and
consequent alteration of the occlusal plane.
Case 1: Surgical prediction tracing involving
clockwise rotation of the maxillomandibular
complex
The presurgical cephalometric analysis of the patient in
case 1 is seen in Fig 3-39a. The diagnosis is Class II malocclusion, maxillary anteroposterior deficiency, macrogenia,
and mandibular alveolar anteroposterior deficiency.
Figure 3-39b illustrates a conventional surgical
prediction involving maxillary advancement, mandibular
advancement, and reduction genioplasty. However, it is
not possible to achieve both good esthetic chin contour
and an acceptable anteroposterior position by means of a
reduction genioplasty. The alternative of clockwise rotation of the maxillomandibular complex can be tested by
means of a surgical cephalometric prediction.

108

b

Step 1
Draw lines on the original tracing representing the
intended osteotomies on the maxilla, mandible, and
symphysis. Where no vertical change of the chin is indicated, the line is drawn horizontally with the mental nerve
and root apices of the mandibular teeth in mind. However,
this case requires a vertical increase as well as anteroposterior reduction. The osteotomy line, therefore, should
be angulated downward. For accurate measurement, the
osteotomy lines should be drawn as close as possible to
the anatomical positions where the osteotomies will be
performed (Fig 3-39c).
Step 2
Place a piece of acetate over the original tracing, and trace
all the structures that will not be altered by the surgery.
This will be the prediction tracing (Fig 3-39d). The ideal
position of the maxillary incisor edge should be indicated
on the prediction tracing by a “box,” where the horizontal
line indicates the ideal vertical position of the incisor tip
and the vertical line indicates the ideal anteroposterior
position of the anterior tooth surface relative to the upper
lip. The desired anteroposterior position of the chin should
also be indicated by a vertical line. Helpful markers for
determining this line include the angle of facial convexity
and the 0-degree meridian; the clinician’s judgment is also
paramount (Fig 3-39e).

Remove the prediction tracing from the original tracing,

osteotomy line. Move the acetate (to the left, in this case) to

Step 4
Place the prediction tracing over the original tracing. The
maxillomandibular complex tracing can now be moved
between the original tracing and the prediction tracing.
In this case, the tracing is rotated in a clockwise direction,
advancing the maxilla and rotating the mandible posteriorly using the “box” for the maxillary incisor tip and
the vertical line for the chin as guidelines. In this case,
the clinician should keep in mind that a reduction genioplasty is indicated to correct the macrogenia and increase
the vertical height of the symphysis. When a satisfactory
position has been achieved, trace the maxillomandibular
complex on the prediction tracing. The part of the symphysis below the genioplasty osteotomy line, however, is not
traced at this time (Fig 3-39h).

Step 5
Remove the maxillomandibular complex tracing, and
superimpose the prediction tracing on the original tracing.
Draw the soft tissue prediction using the same principles
used for conventional prediction of soft tissue response
to hard tissue change (Fig 3-39i). Complete the prediction
tracing by tracing the ideal soft tissue of the chin and the
required reduction genioplasty (Fig 3-39j). Note that in
this instance, a much smaller reduction is indicated than
in the conventional prediction (see Fig 3-39b), resulting
in better soft tissue chin contour.
Step 6
Evaluate the predicted esthetic and functional results
shown on the completed surgical prediction tracing (Fig
3-39k). Changes may be indicated to improve the results.
In the case demonstrated here, the magnitude of the chin
reduction can be reduced by increasing the rotation of the
maxillomandibular complex, keeping in mind the effect of
the increased advancement of the maxilla. The surgical
prediction tracing, however, should always stay within the
limitations of sound surgical technique.

the original tracing and the prediction tracing cross (see
Fig 3-39k). This is the point around which the maxillomandibular complex will be rotated during surgery. The exact

Immediate Preoperative Model
Surgery
The development of CBCT and its application to orthognathic surgery has enabled the clinician to plan corrective
surgery accurately in three dimensions. With reductions
in the cost of hardware and the radiation dose, 3D imaging
has become available for a growing amount of clinicians
and is currently a standard tool for diagnosis and treatment
planning in several disciplines. Although 3D planning is
not an absolute requirement for single-jaw surgery, it has
become essential for accurate planning for the correction
of craniofacial deformities, facial asymmetries, total TMJ
replacement, hemifacial microsomia, and complex rotations of the facial skeleton in three planes. An intermediate

surgical splint made by CAD/CAM or 3D printing is the
link between the virtual simulation and the surgeon in the
operating room.
The author is of the opinion that the basis for any orthognathic surgical planning starts with the examination of the
presurgical dental casts and the visualization of the planned
occlusion (fit of the teeth). Experience gained from conventional cephalometric analysis and model surgery forms the
basis for understanding 3D visual planning.
The primary goal of model surgery is to functionally and
spatially simulate the patient’s jaws and dental structures
as accurately as possible to allow accurate simulation of
the intended surgery. The preoperative position of these
structures can be measured and recorded. The surgical
movement of the jaw(s) or dentoalveolar segments, as
indicated by cephalometric prediction tracings, is simulated on the casts, and the specific spatial changes are then
recorded. In short, model surgery is the dental cast version
of cephalometric prediction of surgical results.
The first step in defining the patient’s deformity in three
planes of space is to place the dental casts on an anatomical
articulator, using a facebow transfer in centric occlusion. The
plaster is trimmed to simulate the anatomy of the maxilla and
mandible as closely as possible. Reference lines are then drawn
on the mounted casts to record their positions in three planes
of space. The second step is to determine the desired final position, aided by the cephalometric prediction tracing and clinical

111

3 | Diagnosis and Treatment Planning

a

b

Fig 3-41 (a) Surgical repositioning of the mandible should take place along the mandibular occlusal plane. The
osteotomy line on the cast should therefore be made parallel to the mandibular occlusal plane to allow the cast to
slide along this plane. Clockwise and counterclockwise rotations at the surgical site can be detected and recorded.
(b) The mandibular cast has been advanced to establish an occlusion. The movements can now be recorded.

data. During the third step, premovement and postmovement
measurements are compared. The clinician can now accurately
determine what dimensional movements will be necessary to
accomplish the intended surgical goals. The surgical options
fall into three basic categories: (1) mandibular surgery only, (2)
maxillary surgery only, and (3) two-jaw surgery.

Mandibular surgery only
When mandibular surgery alone is contemplated, the
mandibular anterior or posterior movement or rotational
movements are dictated by the maxillary dentition. The
occlusion of the mandible is adapted to that of the maxilla.
For example, in mandibular advancement, model
surgery would proceed as follows:
1. Draw a horizontal “osteotomy” line parallel to the
mandibular occlusal plane (Fig 3-41a).
2. Draw vertical reference lines from the cusps of the molars,
canines, and central incisors to the base of the cast (see
Fig 3-41a).
3. Measure the length of the vertical lines, and record the data.
4. Cut the mandibular cast on the horizontal osteotomy line.
5. Advance the cast into the most favorable dental occlusion (Fig 3-41b).
6. Measure anteroposterior vertical and/or rotational movements, and compare them with the premovement data.

Maxillary surgery only
When maxillary surgery alone is contemplated, the mandibular dentition dictates the position of the maxilla. If any
vertical changes of the maxilla are planned, the mandible
will autorotate, which in turn will affect the anteroposterior position of the maxilla. The anteroposterior position is

112

therefore dictated by the mandible whereas the vertical position can be altered by vertical repositioning of the maxilla.
A basic rule is that the postoperative position of the operated jaw is determined by the unoperated jaw, except in the
vertical plane when the maxilla is the jaw to be repositioned.
Following are the procedures to be used in the case of
maxillary repositioning (advancement in this case):
1. Draw a horizontal osteotomy line as close as possible to
where the actual Le Fort I osteotomy will be performed
(Fig 3-42a).
2. Draw two horizontal lines, one line 5 mm above the osteotomy line and one line 5 mm below it (the two lines
are 10 mm apar; see Fig 3-42a). The lateral walls of the
maxilla are not parallel and taper downward. This situation often leads to telescoping, especially of the posterior
maxilla, which limits bone contact, accurate surgical
positioning, and adequate bone fixation. By measuring
the distance between the horizontal lines close to the
osteotomy line, a more accurate assessment of the necessary amount of bone removal can be made (Fig 3-42b).
3. Draw vertical lines from the buccal cusps of the teeth
to the base of the cast.
4. Measure the length of the vertical lines, and record
the data.
5. Cut the cast along the osteotomy line.
6. Perform anteroposterior maxillary repositioning:
a. Advance the cast into the planned occlusion (Fig 3-42c).
b. If no vertical changes of the face are contemplated,
keep the articulator pin fixed and the vertical height
maintained.
c. Trim the casts to fit into position, if necessary.
d. Remeasure the vertical and horizontal distances to
record the required surgical movement.

Immediate Preoperative Model Surgery

Fig 3-42 (a) The osteotomy line is drawn parallel to the occlusal plane. Failure to keep this line parallel to the occlusal plane will lead to vertical lengthening or
shortening of the maxilla as it is advanced. For more accurate measurement, trimming of plaster should be limited to between the two lines drawn 5 mm above
and 5 mm below the osteotomy line. (b) The discrepancy in measuring actual vertical height and measuring the tapering maxillary surface is demonstrated.
(c) Maxillary cast in the planned position. Surgical changes can be recorded. (d) Trim the superior aspect of the cast. Measure the distance from the maxillary
incisor tip to the base of the cast to ensure that the required vertical repositioning has been achieved. Measuring the distance between the horizontal lines
can establish the exact amount of bone to be removed at the anterior and posterior maxilla. (e) Open the articulator until the correct amount of vertical
increase has been achieved. (Measure from the incisor tip to the base of the cast.)

Vertical maxillary repositioning
Another example of maxillary surgery only is vertical
maxillary repositioning. Following are the procedures to
be used for model surgery in such cases:
• Superior repositioning of the maxilla
1. Loosen the articulating pin to allow free movement.
2. Trim the cast to allow the necessary superior repositioning.
3. Place the cast into the planned occlusion, and
measure the vertical height from the incisor edge
to the base of the cast to ensure that the required
vertical reduction has been achieved (Fig 3-42d).
4. Tighten the articulator pin.
5. Wax the cast into position.
6. Remeasure the vertical and horizontal distances, and
record the changes required at surgery (see Fig 3-42d).

• Vertical increase of maxillary height (downgrafting)
1. Loosen the articulating pin to allow free movement.
2. Place the cast into the planned occlusion.
3. Open the articulator while monitoring the vertical
increase from incisor tip to the base of the cast (Fig 3-42e).
4. Once the desired vertical increase has been obtained,
tighten the articulator pin.
5. Wax the cast into position.
6. Remeasure the vertical and horizontal distances,
and record the surgical movements (see Fig 3-42e).

Two-jaw surgery
Planning two-jaw surgery involves the most complex combination of procedures; therefore, a systematic approach is
essential. After careful clinical, cephalometric, and occlusal
analysis, the surgeon faces the following decisions:

Fig 3-43 (a) Preoperative cephalometric tracing. (b) Surgical visual treatment objective. The surgical treatment plan consists of a three-piece Le Fort I maxillary
osteotomy with 5-mm superior repositioning of the anterior segment, 6-mm superior repositioning of the posterior segment, and 5-mm advancement; expansion of

• Position of maxillary incisors. This decision involves the
anteroposterior position (lip support), vertical position
(tooth-lip relationship), cant of the occlusal plane (transverse and horizontal), and dental midline (facial midline).
• The occlusal plane and the vertical position of the
posterior maxilla. This decision is determined by the
position of the mandible after autorotation unless
either manipulation of the occlusal plane is contemplated or a transverse cant of the mandibular occlusal
plane is present and should be corrected.
• Most ideal anteroposterior relationship of the mandible. Once the mandible has been autorotated to the
ideal vertical relationship to the new maxillary incisor
position, it can be advanced or set back along the new
occlusal plane to its most ideal anteroposterior relationship to the maxillary incisor.
• Coordination of anteroposterior changes of the left and
right sides of the maxillary arch with the mandibular
arch rotations.
• Evaluation of transverse arch discrepancies (arch
widths) and their correction (by posterior segmental
model surgery) once the mandibular position has been
established.

cephalometric tracing shown in Fig 3-43a consisted of the

Step 1
Articulate the casts on an anatomical articulator in centric
occlusion using a facebow recording (Fig 3-43c). Trim the
casts to resemble the true anatomy of the maxilla and mandible as closely as possible. Make a duplicate cast of the mandibular dentition. Remove the articulated mandibular cast from
the articulator, and articulate the duplicate cast in exactly the
same way. Change the casts again, and keep the duplicate cast
for use in the construction of an intermediate splint.
Step 2
Draw reference lines:
1. Draw the tooth roots on the casts using the panoramic
radiograph as a guide. Pay special attention to the teeth
adjacent to the interdental osteotomy (Fig 3-43d).
2. Draw a line high on the maxillary cast and a line low on
the mandibular cast parallel to the respective occlusal
planes. These lines will serve as references for vertical
measurements (Fig 3-43e).

3. Perpendicular to each horizontal line, draw vertical
lines to the buccal cusps of the teeth (Fig 3-43f).
4. Draw osteotomy lines representing the actual planes
along which surgical movements will take place. It is
important, especially on the maxillary cast, to draw the
osteotomy line at the same angle and plane at which
the actual Le Fort I osteotomy will be performed. The
mandibular osteotomy line (a sliding plane) should be
parallel to the mandibular occlusal plane (Fig 3-43g).
5. Draw two horizontal lines the same distance (eg,
5 mm) above and below the osteotomy lines. Measure
the distance between them (eg, 10 mm; Fig 3-43h; see
Fig 3-42b).
6. Draw two vertical lines, one on each side of interdental
osteotomies. Measure the distance between these lines
(see Fig 3-43h). They should be drawn on a plane so that
bone can safely be removed without risk of injury to the

roots of the teeth next to the interdental osteotomies.
The lines will act as the limits of safe bone removal
during model surgery.
7. Measure the distance between the buccal surface of the
maxillary incisor and the vertical pin of the articulator
(Fig 3-43i).
8. Draw transverse lines on the palatal surface of the
maxillary cast from left to right, joining lingual cusps
of molars, premolars, and canines. Measure these
distances, and record them for use when transverse
movements (expansion, narrowing, or differential
movement of left and right maxillary segments) are
contemplated (Fig 3-43j).
9. Record all the measurements on the cast. Because some
of the recorded data may be lost during handling of the
articulated casts, these measurements should also be
recorded on a separate form.

Step 3
Using a thin saw, cut the casts along the osteotomy lines.
The interdental cuts should not exceed the guiding lines
(Fig 3-43k). Also, complete the palatal cut to allow expansion of the posterior segments (Fig 3-43l). The articulator
pin should still be tightened. If more than 5 mm of superior repositioning is contemplated, a few millimeters of
plaster should be removed from the maxillary cast base
(Fig 3-43m).
Step 4
Position the anterior maxillary segment according to the
cephalometric visual treatment objective (on which the
postoperative anteroposterior and vertical positions of
the maxillary central incisor have been planned; see Fig
3-43f). This is done by removing plaster from the plaster
segment and using measurements from reference lines
and the articulator pin. The segment is then waxed into
position (Fig 3-43n).
Step 5
Loosen the vertical pin of the articulator. Place the mandibular cast on the sliding plane and move it anteriorly while

116

altering the vertical position by closing the articulator
(autorotation). Ideal incisor and canine relationships are
now established, and the mandibular segment is secured
to the base with sticky wax (Fig 3-43o).
Step 6
Trim the superior aspect of the posterior maxillary
segment, and place the segment into position with the
maxillary teeth to fit the mandibular occlusion. If any
transverse discrepancies exist, consider a midline palatal
osteotomy to facilitate either expansion or narrowing of
the posterior maxilla. Trim the cast according to the actual
anatomical structures (Fig 3-43p). Unrealistic movements
planned during model surgery cause problems during the
actual surgery.
Step 7
Measure the vertical and horizontal changes, and record
the surgical movements (Fig 3-43q). Transverse changes
are measured from the palatal cusps (Fig 3-43r). Replace
and tighten the articulator pin.

Fig 3-43 (cont) (q) Vertical changes are measured by remeasuring the vertical lines from the cusps of
the teeth to the horizontal lines (A). For accurate measurement of the amount of bone to be removed,
the distance between the horizontal lines above and below the osteotomy line is remeasured (B).
(r) Surgical expansion is measured between the palatal cusps of the teeth. (s) Surgical splint reinforced

A

t

Step 8
The author prefers not to use a final surgical splint. Direct
visualization of the occlusal contact during surgery allows
for a more accurate bite correction to be established than is
possible with use of an acrylic surgical splint. The numerous steps of model surgery and fabrication of a surgical
splint leave the process open to imprecision. Even a small
error made in the beginning of the fabrication process will
be increased in magnitude in each subsequent step, so it
is risky to depend totally on a splint for occlusal accuracy
during surgery. An imprecise surgical splint will translate
into an inaccurate final occlusion that may also compromise the esthetic outcome.
An acrylic splint can now be constructed to secure all
the segments in their planned positions during surgery.
The occlusal surfaces of the casts are covered with separating solution to prevent acrylic from sticking to the
plaster during the curing phase. Mix an adequate amount
of self-curing acrylic. Once the acrylic has reached the
“dough” stage, mold it into a thin roll long enough to
cover the dental arch. The thickness of the roll is critical. If
it is too thick, it will be difficult to remove from the
cast and will tend to fracture the plaster. If the splint is
too thin, however, it could easily fracture at a critical stage
of surgery. When a large palatal expansion is contem-

plated, consider reinforcing the splint with a palatal bar
(Fig 3-43s).
Place the acrylic roll on the mandibular occlusal surface.
Slowly close the articulator, gently forcing the teeth into
the planned occlusion. Trim off any excess acrylic with a
sharp knife. Place the articulator into a pressure pot, and
allow the acrylic splint to cure under pressure. Once it has
cured, remove the splint carefully, and trim and polish it.
Make small interdental holes on the buccal side to facilitate
intermaxillary wire placement. Some surgeons prefer to
keep the splint on the maxilla for some time after surgery.
The interdental holes facilitate fixation of the splint to the
maxilla only.
Step 9
Remove the mandibular cast from the articulator and
replace it with the duplicate cast (see Step 1). An intermediate splint can now be constructed as previously described.
This splint will assist in the accurate positioning of the
maxilla in relation to the unoperated mandible during
surgery (Fig 3-43t). The surgeon may prefer to perform
mandibular surgery first. If this is the case, remove the
maxillary cast, rearticulate the original maxillary cast, and
construct an intermediate splint. This splint will relate the
new position of the mandible to the unoperated maxilla.

3 | Diagnosis and Treatment Planning

Virtual Surgical Planning
Steven Sullivan

Virtual surgical planning (VSP) is an increasingly important aspect of oral and maxillofacial surgery, especially
orthognathic surgery. It is a tool that combines recent technological advances with traditional surgical procedures.
This allows for a more streamlined presurgical workup
and improved surgical accuracy.
Advances in technology have improved the surgical
planning and efficiency of orthognathic surgery. The actual
surgery itself has remained relatively unchanged, but VSP
and other innovations have significantly impacted the
presurgical workup. Historically, surgeons required lateral
cephalometric radiographs and tracings to help predict
surgical outcomes, as discussed in the previous section. In
the 1970s, it was shown that lateral cephalometric radiographs could be digitized to help with predicting soft tissue
outcomes. Early on, there were significant hurdles in the
application of digital technology such as difficulties in
manipulation and inherent human error. However, with
advances in VSP, these difficulties have been minimized.
The CT scan, invented in the early 1970s, was the first
of its kind to visualize anatomical structures in a complex,
3D way that had never been done before. The next major
advancement in the digital and technological landscape
came in 1983 with the invention of 3D printing. Human
anatomy was first milled in 1985, leading to the printing of
stereolithographic models during the 1990s. However, digital orthognathic surgery planning was largely limited to the
academic arena. Although the 1990s saw the commercialization of software that could use 3D imaging for planning
and manipulation, even by the end of the decade there had
still been very few changes in the way orthognathic surgery
was planned because there were significant limitations
in actually transferring the 3D virtual plan to the patient.
Therefore, surgeons still relied on the traditional orthognathic workup using plaster casts, facebow transfers, and
estimation of jaw movements.
In the early 2000s, the first validated system was developed that used 3D virtual imaging for surgical planning, 3D
printing, and interocclusal splints that could transfer the
virtual plan to the patient. The process was further refined
to an all-digital workflow that no longer required plaster
casts, but only a CT data set and digital models. Since this
time, VSP for orthognathic surgery has become increasingly
popular and will hopefully become more affordable.
Traditional orthognathic surgery workups require the
fabrication of acrylic splints fabricated on stone casts,

118

which are mounted on semi-adjustable articulators as
described in chapter 3. Splint fabrication on stone casts
carries inherent risks and error because of the materials
being used and processes in which they are made. Though
very rare, there have been reports of malposition of up
to 5 mm occurring in cases planned this way. Now, these
inherent errors can be minimized with CAD/CAM fabrication of splints.
Stone casts of the arches, centric relation records, and
imaging are still necessary even with the application of
VSP; however, the efficiency is improved by minimizing
the amount of laboratory work that is required during the
presurgical workup phase. Often, the errors that occur
during the presurgical laboratory phase are discovered
intraoperatively. However, with VSP, these errors can be
identified preoperatively and can be adjusted prior to
surgery, thereby reducing operative errors.
Inexperienced surgeons can benefit by using computermanufactured cutting guides to aid the orientation of the
osteotomies. Doing so can help take any uncertainty out
of where to place the osteotomy. These cutting guides are
fabricated preoperatively during VSP and can help reinforce orientation of the bony segments. Designing these
jigs preoperatively can help the surgeon avoid important
anatomical structures such as nerves or major vessels.
They can guide bony reduction and orient cuts for segmental osteotomies. The surgeon must keep in mind that these
additional premanufactured components can add quite
significantly to the case costs and could be shifted from the
hospital to the patient. Typical plate and screw costs for a
two-jaw osteotomy range from $1,200 to $3,000. Adding
cutting guides and premanufactured plates can result in
costs that can exceed $7,000, with little evidence to suggest
there is a discernable difference in clinical outcome.
Surgical simulation also allows the surgeon to precisely
determine where osteotomies are to be placed to have
the best possible outcome, to avoid important structures,
and to enhance the fixation screw locations into areas of
high-quality bone. VSP is rapidly becoming the standard
of care due to its improved accuracy, ease of presurgical
simulation, error identification, and improved intraoperative efficiency.
There have been several studies evaluating the accuracy of orthognathic surgery using VSP and CAD/CAM. It
has been found that the accuracy is very good, with errors
being under 1 mm for linear measurements and 1 degree
for angular measurements. VSP is less accurate, however,
when changing the transverse width of the maxilla and when
predicting the final chin position when doing genioplasty.

Virtual Surgical Planning

There is a developing consensus that VSP and CAD are
more accurate and precise than conventional surgical planning. VSP minimizes the preoperative planning times; however,
both types of planning have similar intraoperative times, and
intraoperative complications are not decreased with VSP. The
differences in clinical accuracy are also small enough to not
present a noticeable difference in clinical outcome.
Over the past 25 years, VSP has slowly become the
standard for treatment planning in orthognathic surgery
and other dentofacial abnormalities. The future of VSP in
orthognathic surgery appears promising. Although technology is always changing, the trend toward more accurate
planning and user-friendly software will be a great asset
for oral maxillofacial surgeons in the future.

VSP workflow
In both VSP and traditional model surgery, a comprehensive physical and radiographic examination is crucial
when doing the surgical planning for the correction of
dentofacial deformities. Once a treatment plan has been
developed, surgical movements are carried out with either
traditional model surgery with dental stone casts or virtually using computer programing. Precise surgical splints
are fabricated and used to transfer the proposed surgical
movements into the operating room.
In order to understand the advantages that VSP offers,
one must understand the workflow of traditional model
surgery as discussed in the previous sections. Whether using
traditional methods or VSP, single-jaw surgery does not
require splints to be fabricated from formal model surgery or
digital planning. Splints are unnecessary if a stable occlusion
can be obtained with tooth-to-tooth contact, so single-jaw
surgery will only require a final interocclusal surgical splint
if the final occlusion is unusual or unstable. A segmented
maxilla will often require a palatal splint to maintain the
width change and arch form. Double-jaw surgery will require
an intermediate repositioning splint and possibly a palatal splint. When both jaws are being moved, a stable relationship in the intermediate position is essential, so model
surgery of digital planning is required.
VSP is very similar to the first appointment with traditional model surgery. Necessary preoperative information
is obtained to develop an accurate diagnosis and treatment
plan. This includes preoperative photographs, a CT scan,
physical measurements, alginate impressions, and a centric
relation record. A facebow registration is not necessary
for VSP. Traditionally, a crucial aspect of obtaining the CT
scan had been verifying that the patient was in a neutral

position and in centric relation. There have been multiple
ways that have proven effective at obtaining a natural head
position. These include taking CT scans with the patient
biting on a facebow attached to gyroscope and using a
facebow that has numeric values so the pitch, cant, and
yaw can be calculated. Thanks to the improvements in CT
technology, the process now includes obtaining a CT scan
with the patient biting on a centric relation record and
then the patient’s FH plane is normalized.
The alginate impressions are poured up in die stone
similar to traditional model surgery, and the final occlusion
will be set. Due to scatter that often distorts the occlusal
surfaces of teeth during the CT scan, there must be an
accurate method of capturing the occlusal surfaces. This
can be done with a CT scan of radiopaque stone or through
laser scanning of the die stone casts. The surgeon then
will set the final occlusion and provide the virtual planner
with the clean preoperative occlusion and final occlusion.
One advantage of having the surgeon set the occlusion by
hand with a plaster cast is the appreciation of interdigitation,
occlusal nuances, and anatomy by the surgeon. The virtual
bite will be merged with the CT scan of the skull by superimposing the digital imaging and communications in medicine
(DICOM) data. Once this step is complete, an accurate skeletal and dental depiction of the patient is available (Fig 3-44).
The next step for the surgeon is the planning phase with
office-based planning software or via an online meeting
between a biomedical engineer and the surgeon. When
using an online biomedical engineer, a formulated treatment plan prior to the meeting will expedite the process.
This can be accomplished with a traditional acetate tracing or office-based computer software, and those surgical plans become prescriptive in the online meeting. The
initial steps in the 3D planning process involve the proper
orientation of the 3D rendering of the patient’s skeletal
and dental anatomy. This is confirmed by the proper alignment of the FH plane. Key anatomical landmarks are now
identified and marked for reference, and cephalometric
measurements are recorded (Fig 3-45).
The virtual surgery now begins with the surgeon defining and refining the planned osteotomies. With the final
occlusion set and the maxilla and mandible coupled, the
first movement the surgeon performs is correction of
maxillary midline and cant. Anterior-posterior maxillary
incisor movements and then vertical maxillary movements
follow this (Fig 3-46). Changes in pitch or the occlusal
plane can be made to refine the final chin position and to
assess the need for genioplasty (Fig 3-47).

of the amount of bony overlap as well as any areas that
may require grafting. Another advantage that VSP has over

Once the planned surgical movements are finalized,
the surgeon must decide the sequencing of the surgery.
Mandibular surgery first tends to be more accurate because
it eliminates the influence that an inaccurate bite regis-

122

Fig 3-49 (a) Intermediate position after mandibular movements with the intermediate splint in place (arrow). With
the counterclockwise rotation, the splint is thicker in the
back in a mandible-first surgery. Conversely, in a maxilla-first
surgery, the splint would be significantly thicker in the front,
sometimes inhibiting the ability for maxillomandibular fixation.
(b) Mandibular interim position from the left side showing
