tration has on the final incisor positions from which the
surgery was planned. Mandible-first sequencing is essential
with counterclockwise movements because intermediate splint thickness can be quite significant and influence
condylar positioning if performed second (Fig. 3-49).
Conversely, with large clockwise maxillomandibular
movements, splints can be quite thick if the mandible is

Recommended Reading

a

b

Fig 3-50 Splint designs, from which STL files will be generated for 3D printing. (a) Palatal splint. (b) Intermediate splint.

operated first. It is imperative that if the maxilla is operated first in either scenario that an extremely accurate
preoperative bite registration is obtained. If there are any
inaccuracies, the final maxillary position may be altered
due to habitual posturing of the mandible because of the
malocclusion. This will result in a maxilla that is posterior
to the planned position.
Surgical splints are designed through computer
programing and then 3D printed (Fig 3-50). Printed splints
have a decreased chance of warping when compared with
acrylic splints and have been shown to be as accurate as
surgeon-fabricated splints using traditional additive methods (if not more accurate).
The previously described VSP workflow is an example
of a traditional web-based system in which the surgeon
coordinates and conferences with a biomedical engineer
who is familiar with the virtual surgical software. With the
advent of more user-friendly software and the increased
availability of 3D printing technology, there is now an officebased approach. In the office-based workflow, the surgeon
and their staff work as the software engineers. They will
scan the dental models and merge the accurate occlusal
registration with the CT scan. Then it will proceed with the
staff or surgeon performing cephalometric analysis, virtual
osteotomies, and the virtual surgery to achieve the surgical
goal. This workflow can then use an in-house 3D printer or
an external laboratory to fabricate the 3D splints.
The increase in popularity of VSP has brought about the
question whether or not VSP is more efficient and more
cost-effective than traditional model surgery. Multiple
studies have tried to address these key questions. Time

studies have compared VSP with traditional methods of
planning. They have investigated the time spent during
the presurgical workup and physical examination, the
time spent in the laboratory preparing and performing
model surgery, and the time spent during a VSP session
and generally found that VSP does save time and ultimately
patient costs once the workflow is mastered. The authors
feel that it is safe to say that VSP is here to stay and offers
many advantages over traditional model surgery, especially
when dealing with asymmetries and custom joint replacement with concomitant maxillary procedures.

Diagnosis and Treatment
of Typical Dentofacial
Deformities

T

his chapter and the following chapter use case
studies to demonstrate individual dentofacial
problems, combinations of problems, and the
nuances involved in treating these patients to
achieve optimal results. Patients with similar skeletal,
soft tissue, and occlusal characteristics are grouped in
description for convenience. Clinicians should remember,
however, that each patient has a unique set of dentofacial
problems requiring a specific treatment response, and a
patient may have more than one deformity.
The following basic deformities and treatment principles for each deformity are discussed: (1) mandibular
anteroposterior deficiency, (2) mandibular anteroposterior excess, (3) maxillary anteroposterior deficiency, (4)
maxillary anteroposterior excess, (5) maxillary vertical
deficiency, and (6) maxillary vertical excess.

Mandibular Anteroposterior
Deficiency
Patients with mandibular anteroposterior deficiency and
Class II malocclusions are without a doubt the largest
single group of surgical orthodontic patients.

Class II, division I malocclusion
Clinical characteristics
Profile view
• Retruded, weak chin
• Deficient mandible that gives the illusion of a large nose
• Short chin-to-throat length
• Obtuse lower lip–chin–throat angle
• Everted lower lip that wedges in behind the incisors
• An upper lip that often appears short, curled, and
protrusive
• Acute labiomental fold
• Increased facial contour angle (convex profile)
Frontal view
• Curled lower lip
• Weak chin with the appearance of a double chin
• Deep labiomental fold

Dental characteristics
• Large overjet
• Usually, an increased overbite and an accentuated curve
of Spee
• Usually, crowding in the mandibular incisor area
• Tendency for maxillary incisor protrusion and spacing
• Angle Class II malocclusion

Treatment
Growth modification is not feasible after adolescence.
Without surgical correction of the jaw relationship, an
orthodontic compromise will consist of the following:

sion can be made. It will be prudent to refer the patient for
a consultation with the surgeon before commencement of
orthodontic treatment. The patient’s parents or family should
be encouraged to accompany the patient.

• Correction of excessive overjet
• Retraction of maxillary incisors (with or without
extraction of first premolars)
• Proclination of mandibular incisors (limited by the
bone thickness in the mandibular symphysis)
• Correction of excessive overbite
• Intrusion of maxillary incisors
• Intrusion of mandibular incisors
• Downward rotation of the mandible by opening the bite

Presurgical orthodontics
The basic presurgical orthodontic goals for patients with
Class II, division 1 malocclusion are as follows:

As with most compromised treatment options, this
nonsurgical correction may be associated with disadvantages such as questionable stability, a compromised
esthetic outcome, increased orthodontic treatment time,
and possible periodontal implications because of the
proclination of the mandibular incisors (Fig 4-1).
At the beginning of treatment, the clinician must decide
whether the treatment approach will be solely orthodontic or
orthodontics combined with surgery. Because the orthodontic
treatments in the two approaches differ substantially, it does
not make sense to tell patients who are beginning orthodontic
treatment, “Let’s see how the treatment progresses; we may
get away without surgery.” The clinician should thoroughly
discuss each treatment option with the patient (and the
family, if applicable) so that an informed treatment deci-

126

• Align both dental arches. Both dental arches should be
aligned with the teeth in the central trough of bone,
establishing harmonious arch forms.
• Position the incisors in their planned anteroposterior and
vertical planes with acceptable cephalometric angulation. It is extremely important to position the incisors
correctly. Incisor position dictates surgical movement
to a great extent, and surgical movement in turn dictates
esthetic results. On the other hand, the clinician should
be cautioned not to over decompensate, which could
jeopardize both dental stability and esthetic outcomes.
• Establish arch compatibility.
Note particularly the orthodontic goal of properly positioning the maxillary and mandibular incisors presurgically
in both anteroposterior and vertical planes of space. The
position of the incisors dictates surgical movement, and
failure to eliminate dental compensation limits surgical
correction. Because of crowding in the incisor area and
a severe curve of Spee, it is often necessary to extract
two mandibular first premolars. However, extractions in

the maxilla are not frequently indicated. If there is some

extraction by gaining additional space through expansion
of the maxillary arch—an adjustment often needed to
accommodate the mandibular arch after surgical advancement. If extraction in the maxilla is deemed necessary,
it may be advantageous to extract second premolars to
enable minimal retraction of incisors. Retraction of these
incisors otherwise would not only limit surgical movement
but also have adverse esthetic effects (loss of lip support
and increased nasolabial angle).
Patients with a Class II, division 1 malocclusion often
have an excessive curve of Spee. It is important not to level
the curve routinely before surgery. Patients with short
faces often have prominent chins and, from an esthetic

point of view, do not tolerate mandibular advancement. In
these patients, it is preferable not to level the curve of Spee
presurgically. Surgical advancement of the mandible will
result in a rotational movement, advancing the mandibular
incisors with only minimal advancement of the chin and
slight increase in lower facial height (see chapter 3). As
a general rule, it is better to level the curve by extrusion
of premolars after surgery in patients with short faces.
The greater the facial height, the greater the likelihood
that leveling should be done presurgically by intrusion of
the incisors Because anteroposterior space is needed for
leveling and alignment, extractions are more likely to be
necessary when a severe curve of Spee is combined with
mandibular incisor crowding (Fig 4-2). Surgical leveling of
the curve may be considered (Figs 4-3 and 4-4).

127

4 | Diagnosis and Treatment of Typical Dentofacial Deformities

Arch compatibility plays an important role in immediate postsurgical stability and gives the surgeon an exact
idea of where to position the mandible when a splint is not
used during surgery. There are three important aspects of
arch compatibility:
1. Intercanine width is crucial. If the maxillary intercanine
width is insufficient, it will not be possible to advance
the mandible into the planned occlusion and anteroposterior position. Because a premature contact may
shift the mandible laterally, a rotated canine may make
it impossible to correct dental midlines. The use of an
acrylic splint at surgery to eliminate this problem does
not make sense because the correct intercanine width
must be achieved eventually. If the correct intercanine
width is achieved before surgery, the surgery will be
more accurate, and tooth interdigitation will improve.
2. The mandibular second molars should be banded and
leveled with the first molars. An overerupted, elevated,
often rotated, unbanded second molar frequently
causes occlusal interference at surgery (Fig 4-5).
3. Similar maxillary and mandibular arch forms should be
established to prevent possible postsurgical crossbites. In
patients with mandibular deficiency, the maxillary arch is
often slightly narrow and will need expansion to accommodate the mandibular arch after advancement. Asymmetric arch forms will also influence the yaw of the jaws.
Orthodontic mechanics
Crowding in the maxillary arch. When the maxillary arch
is crowded and requires tooth extractions, the following
approach is recommended:

• Second premolars should be extracted.
• Headgear may be necessary to support the Class III
elastics.
Crowding in the mandibular arch. When the mandibular
arch is crowded and requires tooth extractions (also see
comments on an accentuated curve of Spee), the following
methods should be used:
• Extraction of premolars may be required. In patients
with crowding, accentuated curve of Spee, and the need
for mandibular incisor retraction, the first premolars
should be extracted. The second premolars should be
extracted when less anteroposterior space is required
for the planned mandibular incisor positioning.

128

Fig 4-5 Leveling of the mandibular arch should include the second molars.
An unbanded molar (arrow) often interferes with establishing the planned
occlusion at surgery.

• Sectional arch retraction of the mandibular canines is
recommended because the anchorage characteristics
are excellent.
• Class III mechanics (eg, Class III elastics) may be
needed depending on the severity of the mandibular
canine malposition, the mandibular incisor position,
crowding, and the curve of Spee.
Noncrowding. When minimal crowding or mild spacing
between the teeth is present, a nonextraction approach
is recommended:
• During the leveling process, the use of Class III mechanics is mandatory.
• As much retraction or uprighting of the mandibular
incisor as possible is achieved with the aid of Class III
mechanics (eg, J-hooks or Class III elastics and maxillary headgear support).
• Maxillary and mandibular arches are coordinated to fit.
When the overjet is not excessive (4 to 5 mm), the patient
is asked to move the mandible forward so that the coordination of the maxillary and mandibular arches can be
checked. However, the best way of checking coordination
is to take rough study casts and hand-simulate the surgical advancement of the mandible. Be sure to coordinate
the overjet with the orthodontic prediction tracing.
Surgical treatment
The surgical technique of choice is the bilateral sagittal split ramus osteotomy, which advances the distal
(tooth-bearing) segment to maximum dental intercuspation. The position of the maxillary and mandibular incisors
controls both the amount the mandible can be advanced
as well as the facial height after surgery. Correct condyle
positioning during surgery is extremely important (see the
discussion of condylar sag in chapter 6, pages 268 to 272).

Mandibular Anteroposterior Deficiency

The chin may still appear deficient after advancement of
the mandible, and an advancement genioplasty may be indicated to improve final esthetics. The clinician makes this decision according to the presurgical visual treatment objective.
Light (2.5- to 3.5-oz) Class II elastics are placed after the
completion of surgery to override proprioception, guide
the teeth into the new occlusion, and establish a solid
occlusion. Only two triangular-fashioned vertical elastics
are recommended in the canine area. Long Class II elastics should be avoided because elastics cannot move the
jaw at this stage, and a surgical mistake (eg, condylar sag
or inability to achieve the planned occlusion) cannot be
corrected by elastics.
Postsurgical orthodontics
The goals of postsurgical orthodontics are to bring the
teeth into final occlusion and maintain them in that position. Treatment usually consists of final leveling of the
curve of Spee, correction of minor crossbites, and final
closure of small extraction spaces.

Case 1
A 16-year-old girl was referred to the orthodontist for the
orthodontic treatment of a Class II malocclusion.
Main complaint
The patient mainly wanted her increased overjet corrected
and also thought that her chin appeared too small.
Medical history
Her medical history was noncontributory.
Clinical examination
1. Soft tissue
• Frontal view (Fig 4-6a)
– Short, deficient chin
– Everted lower lip
– Short lower facial third
• Profile view (Fig 4-6b)
– Convex profile
– Deep labiomental fold
– Deficient mandible
2. Dental (Fig 4-6c)
• Class II, division 1 malocclusion

• Slightly accentuated curve of Spee
• Slightly narrowed maxillary arch
3. Skeletal (Fig 4-6d)
• Anteroposteriorly deficient mandible
4. Radiographic
• Panoramic radiograph
– Four impacted third molars
• Cephalometric radiograph
– Class II, division 1 dental relationship
– Slight maxillary dental protrusion
– Mandibular anteroposterior deficiency
Problem list
• Class II malocclusion
• Mandibular anteroposterior deficiency
• Deep bite
• Slightly accentuated curve of Spee
• Narrow maxillary arch
• Slight protrusion of maxillary incisors
Presurgical orthodontics
The surgical removal of impacted third molars preceded
presurgical orthodontic treatment, which consisted of the
following (Figs 4-6e and 4-6f):

• Maxillary arch: use of lingual orthodontics
– Slight expansion of the maxillary arch to accommodate the mandibular arch after advancement
– Slight retraction of maxillary incisors
• Mandibular arch: leveling of the curve of Spee
– Establishment of a good arch form
– Use of buccal orthodontics in the mandibular arch
Surgical treatment
The surgical treatment for this patient consisted of a bilateral sagittal split ramus osteotomy to advance the mandible.
Postsurgical orthodontics
The surgery was performed after a 10-month period of
orthodontic preparation. The postsurgical orthodontic
treatment, which lasted 4 months, involved finalization of
the occlusion. After band removal, the patient was given
a retainer to wear. Figures 4-6g to 4-6i illustrate the treatment result 8 months after orthodontic band removal.

tion to mandibular advancement. However, augmentation genioplasty is not a substitute for mandibular
surgery. Figure 4-7 illustrates the improved esthetic
result of a patient after both mandibular advancement
and augmentation genioplasty.

4 | Diagnosis and Treatment of Typical Dentofacial Deformities

Class II, division 2 malocclusion
Clinical characteristics
Profile view
• Pronounced “chin button”
• Deep labiomental fold
• Anteroposteriorly deficient and curled lower lip
• Short lower facial height and a mandible that appears
square
• Mandibular plane angle that tends to be low
Frontal view
• Appearance of a short face because of decreased vertical height
• Curled lower lip and a deep labiomental fold
• Usually, well-developed elevator muscles of the mandible

Dental characteristics
• Angle Class II, division 2 malocclusion
• Retroclined maxillary central incisors
• Labially flared maxillary lateral incisors
• Deep overbite, which is both skeletal and dental in
origin
• Excessive curve of Spee
• Gingival tissue irritation behind the maxillary incisors
and sometimes on the labial aspect of the mandibular
incisors because of deep bite
• Tendency toward clicking of the temporomandibular
joints (TMJs), probably related to the anterior locking
effect of the deep bite
Treatment
Presurgical orthodontics
The basic presurgical orthodontic principles apply in
Class II, division 2 malocclusion—namely, the need to
properly position the maxillary and mandibular incisors
(vertically as well as in the anteroposterior plane), the
need to level the curve of Spee, and the need to coordinate the dental arches. It is especially important to tip the
maxillary incisors labially to create a good arch form, lip
support, and a sufficient overjet. In patients with severe
deep bite, it may be difficult to level the curve of Spee in
the mandibular arch, making it necessary to open the bite
with a glass ionomer bite plane cemented to the molars.
Surgical leveling by segmental surgery may be considered,
in which case segmental orthodontic alignment will be
necessary (Fig 4-8).
In patients with a Class II deep bite malocclusion, postsurgical leveling of the curve of Spee is indicated. Advance-

132

Fig 4-8 When a natural step exists in the mandibular arch, the dental arch
may be leveled in segments, followed by surgical leveling. Surgical leveling
has two advantages: No additional anteroposterior arch length is required,
and orthodontic treatment time is shortened.

ment of the mandible before total leveling of the curve will
increase the facial height, and because of the rotation of
the occlusal plane, the chin will not advance as much as
the incisors (Fig 4-9a).
Orthodontic mechanics
Maxillary arch. The maxillary incisors can be leveled and
advanced with a curved archwire or a simple flexible archwire followed by a steel round wire with a pronounced
inverse occlusal curve. A stabilizing archwire should be
fitted as soon as possible. The change in the maxillary
incisor position should be reconciled with the arch length
and lip support.

Mandibular arch. In the mandibular arch, the teeth should
be leveled with the aid of Class III elastics and soft flexible wires, but it is not necessary to completely correct
the curve of Spee. This curve correction can be done
after surgery, especially if there is a deep overbite and an
increase in lower facial height is required. The stabilizing
wire should have the curve of Spee bent into it. Presurgically, a deep curve is advantageous because the anterior
part of the mandible is advanced downward and forward
to create a Class I incisor relationship while the lower
anterior facial height is increased (Figs 4-9b to 4-9d).
Surgical treatment
A few surgical options for the correction of the malocclusion and esthetic improvement may be considered:

• Advancement of the mandible by means of a bilateral
sagittal split ramus osteotomy.
• Advancement of the mandible by means of a bilateral
sagittal split ramus osteotomy combined with a reduction genioplasty to reduce chin prominence. By angling

4 | Diagnosis and Treatment of Typical Dentofacial Deformities

• Advancement of the dentoalveolar segment of the
mandible by means of a total subapical osteotomy of
the mandible. This procedure will maintain the chin
position while advancing the lower lip; however, it is
not performed often because of an increased likelihood
of inferior alveolar nerve damage.
• Rotation of the maxillomandibular complex in patients
with a severely low occlusal and mandibular plane angle
(see the section “Rotation of the Maxillomandibular
Complex” in chapter 5).
The following are a few important points regarding
positioning of the mandible during surgery:
• If rigid fixation is used, the incisors are positioned in
ideal relationship.
• Keep skeletal midlines correct even if it means a slight
dental discrepancy. The orthodontist can correct a
mild dental midline discrepancy but not an incorrect
skeletal midline.
• There is often a tendency for posterior crossbites, so
the crossbites should be kept symmetric. It is much
easier to orthodontically correct small bilateral crossbites than a large unilateral crossbite.

Postsurgical orthodontics
After surgery, patients often have a three-point contact
occlusion and bilateral open bites with the contact points
on the incisors and the second molars (see case 2, Fig
4-10). When a bilateral open bite (created by advancing the
mandible where the curve of Spee has not been completely
leveled) will be corrected by orthodontic movement of
mandibular teeth, the maxillary stabilizing arch is left in
place. The mandibular stabilizing archwire is removed
and replaced by a working wire, and light up-down or
slightly Class II elastics are placed. The elastics serve a
dual purpose: (1) to bring the teeth into a solid occlusion,
and (2) to “override” the patient’s proprioception, which
will tend to keep the mandible in maximum intercuspation.
Postoperative crossbites can be corrected by throughthe-bite elastics. From this point forward, the patient’s
orthodontic treatment will be routine.
Retainer use for these patients is the same as for
patients undergoing routine orthodontic treatment. After
3 to 4 months, retainer use can be reduced to nighttime
only. If the teeth are stable, retainer use may be discontinued after a few more months.

4 | Diagnosis and Treatment of Typical Dentofacial Deformities

Mandibular Anteroposterior Excess
As early as 1907, Edward Angle suggested that the only
way to correct severe Class III malocclusion in adults was
to combine surgery and orthodontics. Before the 1970s,
most clinicians thought that Class III malocclusions were
primarily caused by excessive anteroposterior growth of the
mandible, and most of these malocclusions were corrected
by mandibular setback procedures. However, later studies
indicated that isolated mandibular anteroposterior excess
occurs in only 20% to 25% of patients with Class III malocclusions. Some maxillary skeletal anteroposterior deficiency
is involved in 75% of patients with Class III malocclusions.
Therefore, in patients with Class III malocclusion, the clinician must determine whether one jaw is primarily at fault
or the malocclusion is caused by a combination of maxillary
deficiency and mandibular excess.

Class III malocclusion
Clinical characteristics
Profile view
• Long chin-throat length with a well-defined inferior
border of the mandible
• Protrusive chin resulting in a prominent lower third
of the face
• Reduced labiomental fold
• Acute lip-chin-throat angle
Frontal view
• Appearance of a “flat” lower third of the face
• Chin button that is not prominent
• Thin upper lip with reduced vermilion exposure
• Reduced labiomental fold
• Mandible that appears strong
• Asymmetric chin

Dental characteristics
• Often, minimal attached gingival tissue over the
mandibular anterior teeth
• Mandibular incisors that are often compensated and
lingually inclined (however, some patients have generalized interdental spacing and flared incisors, often
combined with an anterior open bite; these characteristics may point to a large tongue)
• Class III malocclusion with anterior and posterior
crossbites
• Often, a dental midline discrepancy

138

Treatment
The timing of treatment for adolescents with Class III
malocclusion is important. Many patients with severe
Class III dentofacial deformities want definitive treatment
as soon as possible, and early treatment may be desirable
from a social and psychologic point of view. In patients
with particularly severe deformities, early intervention
may be attempted to induce growth modification (ie,
reverse-pull headgear). However, if surgery is performed
before completion of mandibular growth, the malocclusion
is likely to recur as a result of growth. The growth of the
patient can be evaluated by a study of a hand-wrist radiograph; however, a serial cephalometric radiograph is a more
accurate tool for monitoring the growth of the mandible.
Maxillary skeletal maturity is usually achieved at age
14 or 15 years in girls and age 16 years in boys; therefore,
when maxillary deficiency is the primary problem, delaying surgery until completion of growth is usually a feasible
option. However, mandibular growth may continue until the
early 20s. Some patients with severe mandibular excess are
not willing to wait and elect to have surgery earlier, recognizing the fact that a second procedure may be necessary later.
The decision to proceed with surgery before the completion
of growth is complex and should not be made lightly. Case
6 (see page 144) is an example of a patient to whom surgery
was offered before the completion of growth.
Presurgical orthodontics
Orthodontic preparation for surgery has five basic goals:

1. Eliminate (or reduce) anterior and posterior dental
compensation with guidance from the orthodontic
visual treatment objective.
2. Establish appropriate anteroposterior and vertical incisor positions. Improper incisor positions will curtail
optimal esthetic correction.
3. Achieve compatible arch forms and intercanine widths,
which are essential to make dental midlines compatible
during surgery.
4. Manage tooth size discrepancies.
5. Correct the mandibular asymmetries that often accompany
mandibular prognathism. If the chin shape is acceptable, the
mandibular dental midline should be placed in the middle
of the chin. The asymmetry will be corrected during the
mandibular setback procedure. It also can be corrected by a
genioplasty, if one is indicated, to improve poor chin shape
or vertical or anteroposterior chin deformities. Thus, it is not
always necessary to orthodontically place the mandibular
dental midline in the midline of the chin.

Mandibular Anteroposterior Excess

Orthodontic mechanics
Mandibular arch. The following techniques are recommended for orthodontic treatment of the mandibular arch:

• Class III mechanics, including molar tiebacks, are not
used when leveling, and the teeth are allowed to level
forward. The orthodontic visual treatment objective
should be referred to regularly to confirm the extent
of incisor decompensation required.
• Upon completion of leveling, Class II elastics may be
used to advance the mandibular buccal segments and
further procline the mandibular incisors. This movement
should be monitored on sequential cephalometric films.
• Arch length discrepancies should be reconciled with
the desired mandibular incisor position.
• When decompensating the mandibular incisors,
the clinician should bear in mind that patients with
mandibular anteroposterior excess often have a very
thin, bony symphysis and a small area of attached
gingiva in the incisor region. Take care to place the
mandibular incisors in the middle trough of the bone.
Maxillary arch. For orthodontic treatment of the maxillary
arch, the following approach is recommended:
• High-pull headgear should be applied to the maxillary
incisor area nightly. This should be used with Class II
mechanics to prevent extrusion of the maxillary incisors when advancing the mandibular dentition.
• Headgear anchorage should aid retraction of the maxillary incisors or merely prevent their proclination during
the leveling process.
Surgical treatment
Bilateral sagittal split osteotomy is the procedure of choice,
although a transoral vertical ramus osteotomy may be indicated in patients requiring large setback procedures. Correct
positioning of the condyle is very important. The surgeon
should carefully free the medial pterygoid muscle and the
stylomandibular ligament from the medial side of the ramus.
Otherwise, the proximal segment will be pushed back by
the distal (tooth-bearing) segment, leading to a backward
rotation of the ramus. With the return of muscle function,
the patient will tend to reposition the mandible forward.
If bicortical screws are used for rigid fixation, positional self-tapping screws are preferred to maintain bone
segment positions. With setback procedures, especially
where simultaneous correction of asymmetry is required,
small intersegmental defects may develop. If these defects

are closed by tightening the screws, the torquing effect
will displace the condyles laterally (peripheral condylar
sag; see the discussion of condylar sag in chapter 6). The
incidence of neurosensory morbidity with transoral vertical ramus osteotomy is less than that associated with a
bilateral sagittal split ramus osteotomy, but the procedure
carries an increased risk of poor condylar control.
On rare occasions, procedures such as body ostectomies or segmental subapical osteotomies are indicated to
correct mandibular anteroposterior excess. A genioplasty
is often indicated to place the chin in its most esthetic
anteroposterior, vertical, and midsagittal positions. The
labiomental fold is often obtuse in patients with Class
III malocclusion; reduction genioplasty would make this
angle more obtuse, causing the chin to appear flat and
resulting in an overall poor esthetic result. Chin shape is
more important than the anteroposterior position of the
soft tissue pogonion (Pog′). A prognathic mandible often
is combined with vertical excess and asymmetry of the
anterior mandible. In these cases, vertical reduction of the
chin, correction of asymmetry, and slight advancement of
the chin may be needed to achieve better chin contour.
Postsurgical orthodontics
Postsurgical orthodontics for patients with Class III
malocclusion is very similar for all patients. In patients
with Class III open bites, the occlusion should be prepared
presurgically so that the only postsurgical orthodontic
tooth movements required are those with little or no
potential to open the bite.
Light (2.5- to 3.5-oz) Class III triangular elastics should be
placed in the canine area to override proprioception in the
immediate postoperative period. A rectangular archwire should
also be placed in the maxilla to prevent molar extrusion.
The clinician should design the retention plan according to the original malocclusion and its possible relapse
tendency.

Continued mandibular growth after surgical
correction
Late or continued growth of the mandible after correction
of Class III cases is an important factor in postsurgical
stability, so the clinician must ensure that facial growth
is completed prior to surgical correction. Serial cephalometric and hand-wrist radiographs are helpful in monitoring growth and estimating whether a growing patient has
experienced his or her growth spurt.
The surgeon may consider performing surgery before the
completion of growth in exceptional cases because “biological
interest is not always in the patient’s psychological interest” (Enlow, 1990). Ages 12 to 19 years are probably the most
formative, and to postpone the correction of severe dentofacial deformities until completion of growth in patients of this
age group often is not best for them psychologically. Surgical
correction should be offered to these patients with the full
understanding of the risk of relapse because of continued
growth. Upon the completion of growth, additional surgical
correction can be performed. Case 6 illustrates a satisfactory
outcome to performing surgery before completion of growth.
Case 6

According to the patient’s parents, her facial appearance
was burdensome because it affected her schoolwork and
social life. She was unhappy and was teased by her peers
about her large mandible. She was also concerned about
the fact that the unfavorable jaw relationship caused an
increasingly severe speech impediment.

Her medical history was noncontributory.

– Unsupported upper lip that appears flat
– Prominent chin
– Increased lower facial height
• Profile view (Fig 4-14a)
– Mandibular anteroposterior excess
– Maxillary anteroposterior deficiency
– “Gummy smile”
– Increased maxillary incisor exposure
– Increased lower facial height
– Concave profile

144

2. Dental (Fig 4-14b)
• Class III malocclusion
• Compensated mandibular incisors
• Posterior crossbites
• Canines partially blocked out
3. Skeletal (Fig 4-14c)
• Maxillary vertical excess
• Maxillary anteroposterior deficiency
• Mandibular anteroposterior excess

Postsurgical orthodontics
Postsurgical orthodontic treatment entailed finalization
of the occlusion and retention. In cases similar to this
one, the postsurgical skeletal change should be accurately
monitored and the dentition not compensated for any
relapse or further growth. When surgery is performed early
and further excessive mandibular growth is expected, the
dentition should not be orthodontically compensated for
this growth because (1) it will disguise the skeletal growth
and (2) the teeth will need to be decompensated again
before final surgical correction.
The postoperative results are demonstrated in Figs
4-14h and 4-14i. Postsurgical growth is evident in Figs 4-14j

to 4-14l (10 months after surgery) and Figs 4-14m to 4-14o
(4 years after surgery). Five years after the initial correction, the clinician decided to perform the final correction.
After a short period of orthodontic treatment to slightly
decompensate the mandibular incisors (Figs 4-14p to
4-14r), the mandible was set back by means of a bilateral
sagittal split osteotomy. The final result is illustrated in
Figs 4-14s to 4-14u (1 year after the second surgery). This
patient certainly benefited from early surgery, even though
a second surgical procedure had to be performed 5 years
after the initial correction. The fact that the bicortical
screws had to be removed prior to performing the bilateral
sagittal split osteotomy made the second surgery challenging. To facilitate this procedure, the use of resorbable
fixation at the first surgery was indicated.

Maxillary Anteroposterior
Deficiency

Class III malocclusion

Maxillary anteroposterior deficiency has often been misdiagnosed as mandibular anteroposterior excess because
of the similarity in appearance. Therefore, the clinician
must carefully distinguish between the two deformities.
In most patients with Class III malocclusion, the deformity is caused by a combination of the two deformities.
As many as 75% of patients with Class III malocclusion
have some degree of maxillary skeletal deficiency. If there
is any doubt about which jaw should undergo surgery, the
maxilla should be advanced.

Profile view
• Sunken cheeks
• Chin and lower lip in balance with the nose
• Sunken or flat appearance of the upper lip
• Reduced upper lip length and thin vermilion
• Frequently, an acute nasolabial angle with the columella
of the nose oriented more horizontally

Clinical characteristics

Frontal view
• Flat and relatively short upper lip
• Often, a narrow alar base
• Often, sclera seen inferiorly of the iris of the eye

147

4 | Diagnosis and Treatment of Typical Dentofacial Deformities

•
•
•
•

Sunken cheeks
Normal to deficient maxillary tooth-to-lip relationship
Less vermilion exposure of the upper lip
Paranasal flattening

The relationship of each individual jaw to the cranium
can be evaluated clinically by blocking out the mandible
and then the maxilla with a hand or card (see Fig 2-23 in
chapter 2).
Dental characteristics
• Class III malocclusion
• Often, crowding in the maxillary arch
• Often, small or absent maxillary lateral incisors
• Often, normal inclination of mandibular incisors in
comparison with the lingual inclination seen in mandibular anteroposterior excess
• Tendency of the maxillary arch to be narrow and often
in lingual crossbite with the mandibular arch
Treatment
Presurgical orthodontics
The treatment of maxillary anteroposterior deficiency has
the same basic goals as does the treatment of mandibular
anteroposterior excess:
• Eliminate compensations
• Establish ideal incisor position (indicated by the orthodontic visual treatment objective)
• Establish arch compatibility
• Level and align arches
It is important to keep in mind that the transverse
discrepancy that often accompanies the maxillary anteroposterior deficiency may have to be corrected by surgical
expansion of the maxilla. If this procedure is contemplated,
the arch should be aligned accordingly and the roots of the
teeth next to the interdental osteotomy should be deviated.
Two-jaw surgery may be indicated in patients with severe
Class III malocclusion. Here the orthodontist should adopt the
“two-patient” concept, in which the mandibular and maxillary
arches are treated independently, almost as if they belong to
two different patients; however, the two arches should still be
compatible. The objective is to align the maxillary and mandibular incisors in both vertical and anteroposterior planes of
space, so the surgeon can achieve optimal skeletal and esthetic
correction without the limitations of dental interference.
Patients with maxillary deficiency often have tooth
crowding in the maxilla, and retraction of incisors is indi-

148

cated. Retraction will necessitate extractions, which follow
these principles:
• If maximum retraction is necessary or significant
crowding is present, removal of maxillary first premolars is indicated.
• If little retraction is necessary and crowding is slight,
removal of second premolars is indicated.
• Advancement of mandibular incisors from an upright
or lingually tipped position may be limited by lack of
attached gingiva and/or a thin alveolar bone and symphysis. Mandibular second premolar extraction may be necessary to provide the required space to manage crowding.
• The most common extraction pattern in patients
with Class III malocclusion is extraction of maxillary
first premolars and, when extraction in both arches is
indicated, extraction of maxillary first premolars and
mandibular second premolars (the opposite of Class
II malocclusion).
Orthodontic mechanics
Crowding is often present in the maxillary arch and
requires tooth extraction. The canines are usually blocked
out, which is often the patient’s main complaint before
treatment. The decision whether to extract the first or
second premolars is influenced by the amount of crowding
and required decompensation of the incisors. Presurgically,
the maxillary incisors should be placed in good angulation
in the central trough of bone. To achieve the best esthetic
result, anteroposterior positioning of these teeth should
be based on the visual treatment objective. Decompensation of the mandibular incisors is seldom indicated. The
mandibular arch should be leveled and aligned to act as
the “template” for the maxillary dental arch.
Surgical treatment
The maxilla is advanced by means of a Le Fort I osteotomy. This versatile procedure enables the surgeon to
correct discrepancies in the anteroposterior, vertical, and
transverse planes. Grafting is recommended for large
advancements and expansion of the palate. Various grafting materials are available, including bone harvested from
local or distant sites such as the chin or iliac crest; allogeneic freeze-dried bone; and artificial bone substitutes such
as hydroxyapatite blocks.
In the past, undesirable soft tissue changes may have
occurred, including widening of the alar base, tipping up of
the tip of the nose, and an increase in the nasolabial angle.
These esthetically unfavorable effects may still occur in

Maxillary Anteroposterior Deficiency

patients in whom the nasal morphology is not controlled
during surgery. The patient should be informed about
the possibility of soft tissue changes. Control of the nose
during Le Fort I osteotomy is mandatory and described
elsewhere in the text (see the section on Le Fort I maxillary
osteotomy in chapter 6).
Postsurgical orthodontics
Postsurgical orthodontic treatment is very similar to that
for patients with mandibular setback. A splint is used
only when a multi-piece Le Fort I maxillary osteotomy
is performed. In two-jaw surgery, an intermediate splint
is always used to accurately position the maxilla. The
patient will be able to function with the occlusal splint
until adequate healing has taken place and active orthodontics can be resumed.
Ligature wires between teeth adjacent to interdental
osteotomies should be placed by the surgeon and removed
only by the orthodontist during the placement of a continuous archwire.
When surgical expansion has been performed, the
transverse dimension should be maintained by an occlusal
splint reinforced by a palatal bar. The palatal bar prevents
bending of the splint during and after surgery. Once the
splint is removed, the orthodontist should place an orthodontic palatal bar and a continuous archwire as soon as
possible to further control the transverse dimension.

Maxillary Anteroposterior Excess
Maxillary anteroposterior excess is certainly not as
common as was implied when Class II malocclusions were
classified strictly according to the Angle dental classification. According to McNamara (1981), only approximately
10% of a group of 277 patients with Class II malocclusions
had true maxillary anteroposterior excess. In the past,
anterior maxillary segmental osteotomies were erroneously performed for the correction of Class II mandibular
deficiency, resulting in poor esthetic outcomes.
The clinician should carefully differentiate between
maxillary anteroposterior excess and mandibular anteroposterior deficiency because of the previously discussed

esthetically unfavorable results achieved after the surgical or orthodontic retraction of maxillary incisors in a
patient with mandibular anteroposterior deficiency.
Of course, maxillary anteroposterior excess can occur
in combination with mandibular deficiency (see case 10
later in this section).

Overview
Clinical characteristics
Profile view
• General protrusion of the middle third of the face
• Nose that often appears large and has a dorsal hump
• Prominent infraorbital rims and cheekbones

153

4 | Diagnosis and Treatment of Typical Dentofacial Deformities

• Upper lip that is often short and everted
• Deep labiomental sulcus because the lower lip curls
under the maxillary incisors
• Lip incompetence
• Acute nasolabial angle
Frontal view
• Noticeable prominence of the middle third of the face
• Often, a long lower facial height
• Short, curled upper lip
• Lower lip that is curled under the maxillary incisors

Dental characteristics
• Tendency toward an open bite
• Tendency toward a narrow, constricted maxillary arch
• High, arched palatal vault
Treatment
In the growing child, orthodontic correction is certainly
most effective. It usually involves the use of headgear

154

and multibanded fixed appliance therapy. Depending on
the severity of deformity and tooth crowding, it also may
involve the extraction of four premolars.
In the nongrowing patient, surgery that sets back the
total maxilla or anterior maxillary segment hastens treatment and avoids headgear therapy. Total maxillary setback
is performed at the Le Fort I level and is seldom indicated
and technically difficult.
Surgical treatment
Three surgical techniques for repositioning the anterior
maxilla have been described by Wassmund (1935), Cupar
(1954), and Wunderer (1963). When posterior movement of
the anterior maxillary segment is the main objective, the technique described by Wunderer is the most practical approach.
An anterior segmental osteotomy of the maxilla is often
performed as part of a multisegment Le Fort I procedure
because patients who need anterior maxillary correction
often need additional corrections to the maxilla (eg, superior repositioning and/or expansion of the posterior maxilla).

Maxillary Anteroposterior Excess

a

b

c

Fig 4-19 Inadequate orthodontic root deviation (a) will force the surgeon to either lift the canine superiorly out of the occlusion (b) or tip the incisors
inferiorly (c), increasing the anterior height of the maxilla.
Fig 4-20 (a) Adequate orthodontic deviation of
the roots of the teeth adjacent to the interdental
osteotomy will allow the surgeon to close an interdental space. (b) To ensure the integrity of the
periodontium, the space should never be completely
closed, and a small amount of alveolar bone should
always be left intact at the alveolar crest.

a

The author finds it easier to perform a multi-piece Le Fort I
procedure than an anterior segmental osteotomy (Fig 4-18).
In the presurgical phase, the roots of the teeth on either
side of the osteotomy cut or ostectomy must be deviated
sufficiently. Inadequate deviation of the roots of these
teeth will have the following consequences:
• Limit the surgical setback
• Increase the risk of damage to the tooth roots at surgery
• Force the surgeon to rotate the segment, thereby either
elevating the canines out of occlusion or tipping the
incisors inferiorly (Fig 4-19)
Adequate deviation of the roots allows setback of the
anterior maxillary segment. A small area of interproximal
bone should be maintained and the crowns of the teeth
not forced together (Fig 4-20).
Maxillary setback procedures are seldom indicated.
The clinician contemplating such a procedure should
consider the esthetic effects carefully before making a
final decision.

Maxillary Vertical Deficiency
Maxillary vertical deficiency is very often associated with
maxillary anteroposterior deficiency, in which the maxilla
does not develop in a forward and downward direction. It
is common in patients with cleft lip and cleft palate, where
early surgery often curtails normal development of the
maxilla. Because overclosure of the mandible makes patients
with maxillary vertical deficiency appear clinically similar to
those with mandibular anteroposterior excess, the clinician
should differentiate between the two deformities.

Maxillary Vertical Deficiency

g

h

i

Fig 4-22 (cont) Posttreatment frontal view (g), profile view (h), smile (i), and occlusion (j). Note the
correction of the maxillary midline.

j

Overview
Clinical characteristics
Profile view
• Lower and middle thirds of the face that are proportionally reduced in height when the patient’s teeth are
in occlusion
• Acute nasolabial angle
• Appearance of an excessive chin because of overclosure
• Improvement in the profile when the mandible is in
the rest position
• Maxillary incisors that are not visible under the upper lip
Frontal view
• Appearance of a short and square face, with strong,
exaggerated masseter muscles
• Maxillary incisors that often are not visible with the
mouth open, creating an edentulous appearance, and
incisors that are only partially exposed when the patient
smiles
• When the mandible is closed, corners of the mouth
that are turned down and skin folds that are apparent
lateral to the oral commissure
• Often, a broad nasal base and large nostrils
• Appearance of an excessive mandible

Dental characteristics
• Heavy musculature and overclosed occlusion that
frequently predispose the patient to bruxism and
resulting attrition
• Often, increased interocclusal freeway space
• Class III dental relationship
Treatment
The surgical treatment objective for patients with maxillary
vertical deficiency is to reposition the maxilla forward and
downward. The mandible will rotate clockwise, and the vertical
length of the face will increase. Patients with maxillary vertical
deficiency invariably have an increased interocclusal freeway
space, which should be carefully evaluated. The increase in
vertical height achieved by downgrafting the maxilla should be
less than the “available” interocclusal freeway space.
The extent of the vertical deficiency can be measured
only with the patient’s lips in repose and almost touching.
Pretreatment cephalometric radiographs should be taken
with the mandible in the rest position and the lips barely
touching. This positioning can be facilitated by the use of
a wax splint. The cephalometric visual treatment objective
should be created on a radiograph taken with the mandible
rotated open and the lips barely apart. This radiograph
will give the surgeon a good indication of (1) the required
amount of maxillary downgrafting to achieve an ideal
lip-tooth relationship and (2) the mandibular anteroposterior position following clockwise rotation.

159

4 | Diagnosis and Treatment of Typical Dentofacial Deformities

Presurgical orthodontics
The objective in presurgical orthodontic treatment for
patients with maxillary vertical deficiency is to (1) level and
align the teeth and (2) coordinate the arches. Significant
anteroposterior compensations are seldom present, and
the incisors can often be left in their original anteroposterior positions. If severe crowding is present, the maxillary
and mandibular first premolars are extracted; with mild
crowding, the maxillary and mandibular second premolars are extracted. Where larger maxillary advancement
is needed in patients with crowding, the first maxillary
premolars and second mandibular premolars are extracted
to create a large crossbite.
If a transverse deficiency of the maxilla is present, it is
best to correct it surgically. The maxillary arch often can
be coordinated in two halves to “fit” the mandibular arch
and the archwire cut in the center. A stabilizing palatal arch
should be placed as soon as possible after surgery, and it
should be anticipated presurgically.
Surgical treatment
The maxilla can be repositioned inferiorly by a Le Fort I
downgrafting procedure. This procedure was unstable in
the past, and as high as a 70% loss of vertical height has
been reported; however, rigid fixation has substantially
improved the stability of the procedure.
Definitive presurgical planning of the exact amount
of maxillary inferior repositioning is critical. The clinician should be guided by both the amount of interocclusal rest space and the maxillary incisor–upper lip
relationship.
After the maxilla is mobilized on the Le Fort I level,
maxillomandibular fixation is placed. The condyles are
seated in the glenoid fossae, and with gentle backward and
upward pressure at the mandibular angles, the maxilla is
rotated closed until the desired, preplanned interosseous
distance is achieved. Four bone plates are placed: two at
the zygomatic buttresses and two at the piriform rims.
An interpositioning graft is then placed (or it may be
placed before the bone plates). This graft is shaped to
fit snugly into the bony defect to prevent displacement
into the maxillary sinus. If the transverse dimension of
the maxilla is increased by surgical expansion, an acrylic
occlusal splint is placed and the palatal bone defect grafted.
An interdental wire is inserted between the teeth adjacent
to the interdental osteotomy.
Patients with a combination of vertical and anteroposterior deficiency may be candidates for the Le Fort I
downsliding osteotomy design. The surgical plan for these

160

patients should include maxillary advancement in addition
to correction of the vertical discrepancy. In these patients,
the osteotomy is angled to provide an inclined plane that
will increase the vertical dimension as the maxilla slides
forward. The horizontal length from the piriform rim to
the lateral aspect of the zygoma is measured on a lateral
cephalogram. This measurement is used to calculate the
downward angulation of the osteotomy and the position
of the vertical steps.
Postsurgical orthodontics
During the first postsurgical visit, the orthodontist checks
the occlusion and instructs the patient to wear appropriate
elastics. If surgical expansion of the maxillary arch was
performed, the orthodontist should do the following:

• Remove the sectional archwire and replace it with a
continuous archwire.
• Place a palatal arch that is carefully adapted to the
lingual surface of all teeth distal to the canines.
At first, the orthodontist sees the patient once every
1 to 2 weeks for routine adjustment of archwires and
elastics. With good progress, the patient goes to a 4-week
adjustment schedule, and postsurgical orthodontic treatment is concluded as previously discussed.

4 | Diagnosis and Treatment of Typical Dentofacial Deformities

Maxillary Vertical Excess
Approximately 30% of patients seeking treatment have a
vertical increase in the lower third of the face. The author’s
experience shows that the main complaints of patients
with vertical maxillary excess are a gummy smile and/or
functional problems caused by the anterior open bite, the
hallmarks of the long-face deformity.

Overview
Clinical characteristics
Profile view
• Increased total facial height caused by an increased
lower facial height
• Mandible that is rotated downward and backward
• Increased interlabial distance (greater than 4 mm)
• Increased maxillary incisor exposure (except in some
patients with an open bite)
• Sunken cheeks
• Often, a well-developed, almost excessive, curled lower lip
Frontal view
• Narrow alar base width
• Excessive maxillary incisor exposure (except in some
patients with an open bite)
• Increased interlabial distance
• Often, increased vermilion exposure of the lower lip
• Increased lower-third facial height
• Gummy smile
• Depressed paranasal areas with a likelihood of flat cheeks

Dental characteristics
• Often, an anterior bite
• High, arched palate with a large distance between the
root apices and the nasal floor
• V-shaped maxilla and teeth often in palatal crossbite
• Mandibular incisors that tend to become more upright
and therefore more crowded
Because of the excessive vertical growth of the maxilla,
the mandible tends to rotate downward and backward.
Patients with maxillary vertical excess therefore often
have problems in the anteroposterior plane. Patients with
long faces can be described as having a skeletal Class I
relationship rotated to Class II or a skeletal Class III relationship rotated to Class I. It can be concluded that excessive vertical growth of the maxilla will make mandibular
anteroposterior deficiencies appear worse and mandibular

164

anteroposterior excesses appear improved. Frequently,
there is an accentuated curve of Spee caused by overeruption of mandibular incisors, especially in patients without
open bites.
There is a tendency for anterior open bites in two-thirds
of these patients. The clinician should note that some
diagnostic characteristics may be camouflaged in patients
with vertical maxillary excess and deep bites (Figs 4-25a
to 4-25g). First, because of the deep bite, the lower facial
height may not be increased. Second, the maxillary incisors
may be excessively exposed under the upper lip; however,
this exposure is covered by the lower lip. The combination
of the following characteristics may be considered diagnostic for vertical maxillary excess:
•
•
•
•
•

Increased mandibular plane and occlusal plane angle
Increased interlabial gap
Increased incisor exposure under the upper lip
Increased total anterior facial height
Decreased percentage contribution of upper facial
height to total facial height

Treatment
The two most important rules in the treatment of vertical
maxillary excess are the following:
1. Never treat the smile.
2. Always plan the treatment with the lips in repose.
There is no alternative to surgery for adult patients
with vertical maxillary excess. Attempts to close the
open bite by orthodontic extrusion of anterior teeth or
intrusion of posterior teeth are not a solution for two
reasons: (1) relapse into open bite almost always occurs,
and (2) extrusion of the maxillary incisors will worsen
the esthetics.
Presurgical orthodontics
As in all surgical orthodontic cases, the approach is to align
the teeth presurgically to facilitate the planned surgery so
that the teeth will fit into occlusion after surgery. A few
guidelines should be considered:

• It is preferable to level the mandibular arch before
surgery (in contrast to the approach in patients with
short faces).
• Patients with vertical maxillary excess and severe
open bites often have an excessive reverse curve in
the maxillary arch. For these patients, it is advantageous

to level the arch in segments and deviate the roots of

• A similar principle applies in the decision to expand
a narrow maxilla orthodontically. The teeth should

not be expanded beyond their bony base. Any relapse
of an expanded maxillary arch will tend to open the
bite anteriorly. Surgical expansion is recommended,
especially in more severely narrowed maxillae and in
older patients.

4 | Diagnosis and Treatment of Typical Dentofacial Deformities

Orthodontic mechanics
Mandibular arch
• Routine orthodontic techniques are used to level, align,
close all spaces, and achieve a good arch form.
• Extractions and Class II or Class III mechanics may be
necessary depending on the patient.

Maxillary arch
• Teeth adjacent to the proposed interdental osteotomies (eg, between maxillary canines and premolars
or between maxillary lateral incisors and canines) are
bonded with brackets that will “diverge” the roots
away from the surgical site to prevent accidental surgical insult to the roots. For example, a maxillary left
canine bracket is placed on the right canine and vice
versa. This technique is still applicable if premolars
are removed to provide relief from crowding. After
surgery is completed, these brackets are removed and
the correct brackets bonded to obtain normal root
inclination.
• Leveling is accomplished using sectional nickeltitanium alloy (nitinol) wires or similar wires. Stabilization is then undertaken with sectional finishing
archwires bent to conform to the arch form for each
segment.
• Extraction spaces can be closed by surgically approximating the segments. Keep in mind that interdental spaces should not be totally closed at the time of
surgery. A small amount of bone should always be
preserved at the tooth margins to prevent periodontal
problems.
• If no vertical segmental discrepancies exist, the maxillary arch is leveled with a continuous archwire. Care
should be taken that the maxillary incisors are not
leveled forward; nightly headgear wear may be required.
The maxillary and mandibular arches are coordinated
with the aid of study casts.
• Intrusive posterior mechanics, such as palatal bars or
high-pull headgear, should be avoided.
• Mandibular molars should be brought upright to
encourage bite opening. This situation will accentuate the vertical problem and introduce less chance of
relapse.
Surgical treatment
To correct the vertical discrepancy, the maxilla must be
superiorly repositioned by a Le Fort I osteotomy. Two
critical elements must be considered in the final treatment
planning:

166

1. How far must the maxilla be superiorly repositioned?
2. After straight vertical repositioning of the maxilla,
where will the mandible be?
Extent of superior repositioning. The amount of superior
repositioning is critical because moving the maxilla too far
superiorly is more detrimental to facial esthetics than leaving the vertical excess uncorrected. Consider the following
points when planning superior repositioning of the maxilla:
• Patients with short upper lips show more teeth than
patients with long upper lips.
• Younger patients tolerate (esthetically and psychologically) more upward movement of the maxilla than
do older patients. Remember that the upper lip will
lengthen with age.
• Exposure of 30% to 40% of the clinical crowns of the
maxillary incisors beneath the upper lip is esthetically
pleasing.
• During superior repositioning of the maxilla, the upper
lip will shorten. The amount of upper lip shortening
is specific to the surgeon and will depend on the individual surgical technique (eg, suturing technique and
careful handling of soft tissue during surgery).
• In general, plan for a 4-mm tooth exposure after surgery.
• It is not mandatory to elevate the maxilla until the lips
make contact. A few millimeters of lip incompetence is
acceptable and, in many instances, attractive.
• Large movements, if not controlled, may lead to widening of the alar base of the nose and tipping up of the
nasal tip. Because both effects are controllable, take
them into consideration in treatment planning and
surgical technique (see the sections on nasal control
in chapter 6, pages 295 and 296).
• Moving the maxilla too far superiorly gives poor
esthetic results, but moving it posteriorly at the same
time yields even worse results.
• Consider the upper lip shape. Patients with an extreme
Cupid’s bow may have excessive exposure of only the
two central incisors. If surgery is planned using the
lateral cephalometric radiograph without considering
the lip shape, treatment may result in only the tips of
the two central incisors being visible under the upper
lip. The maxillary incisor exposure should therefore be
assessed clinically and not based on the lateral cephalometric radiograph (Figs 4-25h to 4-25j).
Anteroposterior position of the mandible. The mandible
will autorotate counterclockwise around a point at the

Fig 4-25 (cont) Assessment from a profile view (h) and a lateral cephalometric tracing (i) reveals an interlabial distance of 10 mm, requiring superior repositioning of the
maxilla. If only this view is considered, superior repositioning of at least 6 mm is indicated. (j) Because of an accentuated Cupid’s bow of the upper lip, the entire crowns of
the central incisors are exposed under the upper lip, whereas there is much less exposure of the lateral incisors. If planning of superior repositioning of the maxilla considers
only the central incisor exposure, excessive superior repositioning will result in only the tips of the central incisors being visible under the upper lip.

condyle. As a consequence, the mandibular incisors will
rotate forward (more so in patients with a high occlusal
plane angle). When the extent of maxillary superior repositioning has been decided, the anteroposterior position
of the mandibular incisors should be considered. It may
be necessary to advance the maxilla slightly to achieve a
Class I malocclusion. If the mandible is anteroposteriorly
deficient, a maxillary setback may be considered to achieve
a Class I malocclusion; however, advancing the mandible is
preferable. It is much better to accept the need for two-jaw
surgery to achieve a good occlusion than to significantly
compromise the esthetics and limit the surgery to one
jaw. In patients with Class III malocclusion, the Class III
dental relationship will worsen as the mandible is autorotated, necessitating either maxillary advancement or
mandibular setback.
Important surgical considerations
• The amount of superior repositioning of the maxilla
is critical. Take great care during surgery to accurately
position the maxilla in the planned position. An intermediate splint is helpful. The amount of superior repositioning of the maxilla should be evaluated from the
frontal view and not the lateral cephalometric radiograph (see Fig 4-18).
• Condyle positioning, again, is critical. The condyle
can be displaced by minor bony interferences in the
posterior maxilla. With maxillomandibular fixation,
this condylar displacement will not be revealed until
6 weeks after surgery, after removal of maxillomandibular fixation. Maxillomandibular fixation is currently

seldom indicated and not often used. With rigid internal fixation, incorrect positioning of the condyle can
be detected after intraoperative removal of maxillomandibular fixation. The occlusion should be critically
evaluated following removal of intraoperative maxillomandibular fixation. If the occlusion differs from
the planned occlusion, the cause for the discrepancy
should be established. Occurrence of an unplanned and
unacceptable occlusion is most often the result of bony
interferences at the posterior maxilla. This problem
should be corrected by the removal of fixation plates
and the elimination of posterior interferences (see the
section on intraoperative diagnosis of condylar sag
during Le Fort I maxillary osteotomy in chapter 6).
• It is often necessary to surgically expand the posterior maxilla through parasagittal osteotomies in the
palate. A bone graft or hydroxyapatite blocks should
be grafted into the defect and the segments stabilized
by a splint with a palatal bar. Grafting should be used
for any expansions greater than 3 mm.
• The piriform rim should be contoured to accommodate
the soft tissue of the nose (see the sections on nasal
control in chapter 6).
• The bony and cartilaginous septum should be shortened appropriately to prevent warping of the nasal
septum during superior repositioning of the maxilla.
To centralize the septum, a septum suture may be used
to attach the septal cartilage to the base of the nasal
spine as it passes through a hole in the nasal spine (see
the sections on nasal control in chapter 6).

167

4 | Diagnosis and Treatment of Typical Dentofacial Deformities

• Wound closure should be meticulous. A cinch suture
can control the alar base width, and V-Y closure can
create lip lengthening or control the length of the upper
lip. Resuture the perioral muscles, and then carefully
suture the mucosa, taking thin bites. Be sure to maintain the soft tissue midline when suturing (see the
sections on nasal control in chapter 6).
Postsurgical orthodontics
If a splint is used during surgery, the orthodontist should
see the patient immediately after splint removal. Stabilizing archwires are removed and replaced with working
archwires, and light (2.5- to 3.5-oz) vertical or slightly Class
II elastics are placed.
After surgical expansion, the most important and possibly the most challenging aspect of postsurgical orthodontics is to maintain the transverse dimension of the palate.
The expanded dentoalveolar segments must be held in
their expanded position during orthodontic finalization.
This can be accomplished using either a heavy auxiliary
wire in the headgear tubes along with working wires or a
transpalatal arch (the latter may be more practical). The
dentoalveolar segments do not stabilize until 4 to 6 months
after surgery. In general, the postsurgical orthodontic
treatment of patients with long faces is often quick because
the arches were leveled either before or during surgery.

Case 14
A 16-year-old adolescent boy was referred for surgical
correction of vertical maxillary excess and mandibular
deficiency. (His mother had the same condition and had
been treated 10 years previously by the same surgeon.)
Main complaint
The patient was unhappy about his gummy smile and his
inability to create a lip seal without strain.
Medical history
His medical history was noncontributory.

1. Soft tissue
• Frontal view (Figs 4-26a and 4-26b)
– Gummy smile
– Increased interlabial gap
– Excessive maxillary incisor exposure under the
upper lip

– Increased lower lip vermilion exposure
• Profile view (Fig 4-26c)
– Convex profile
– Deficient chin
– Prominent lower lip
– Increased lower-third facial height
– Increased tooth exposure
– Increased interlabial gap
2. Dental (Fig 4-26d)
• Class II malocclusion
• Missing first premolars
3. Skeletal
• Vertical maxillary excess
• Mandible rotated clockwise and is relatively deficient
• Microgenia
4. Radiographic
• Panoramic radiograph
– Missing first premolars
• Cephalometric radiograph (Fig 4-26e)
– Confirms clinical observations
Problem list
• Vertical maxillary excess
• Microgenia
• Class II malocclusion
Presurgical orthodontics
The presurgical orthodontic treatment was completed by
the time of the final referral.
Surgical treatment
A Le Fort I maxillary osteotomy was performed to reposition the maxilla superiorly, the mandible was autorotated
counterclockwise into a Class I dental relationship, and a
genioplasty was performed to advance the chin (Fig 4-26f).
Postsurgical orthodontics
Postsurgical orthodontic treatment involved completing the interdigitation of the teeth and the wearing of a
retainer. The pleasing postsurgical result is demonstrated
in Figs 4-26g to 4-26j.

5

Diagnosis and
Treatment of Complex
Dentofacial Deformities

A

s in chapter 4, this chapter uses case studies to
demonstrate individual dentofacial problems,
combinations of problems, and the nuances
involved in treating these patients to achieve
optimal results. Patients with similar skeletal, soft tissue,
and occlusal characteristics are grouped in description
for convenience. Clinicians should remember, however,
that each patient has a unique set of dentofacial problems
requiring a specific treatment response, and a patient may
have more than one deformity.
The following basic deformities and treatment principles
for each deformity are discussed: (1) cases requiring rotation
of the maxillomandibular complex, (2) open bite deformities, (3) temporomandibular joint (TMJ) reconstruction
and orthognathic surgery, and (4) dentofacial asymmetry.

Rotation of the Maxillomandibular
Complex
The correction of most dentofacial deformities requires
single-jaw surgery whereby either the maxilla or the
mandible is repositioned. The mandible can be surgically
advanced or set back, and this horizontal movement should
take place along the occlusal plane of the maxilla (ie, the
unoperated jaw). Surgical repositioning of the maxilla,
either anteriorly or posteriorly (albeit seldom), will also
take place along the existing occlusal plane. However,
whereas superior repositioning of the maxilla will cause the
mandible to rotate counterclockwise, inferior repositioning of the maxilla will result in a clockwise rotation of the

172

mandible. This rotation of the mandibular occlusal plane
occurs around a point at or just behind the head of the
condyle. To achieve occlusal contact, the maxilla must be
aligned along a “new” occlusal plane, which is determined
by the extent of the autorotation of the mandible. Hence, it
is the position assumed by the mandible that dictates the
final anteroposterior cant of the occlusal plane (Fig 5-1).
When corrective treatment requires surgery to both
jaws, the same treatment principles as previously discussed
will apply and are called conventional treatment planning.
Most dentofacial deformities can be treated successfully
using conventional treatment planning, in which alteration of the occlusal plane is an inevitable consequence
of any surgical adjustment to the vertical position of the
maxilla with resultant autorotation of the mandible. Any
anteroposterior repositioning of the maxilla or mandible
must take place along this new occlusal plane (Fig 5-2).
Adhering to conventional treatment principles (especially in patients with high and low occlusal plane angles)
may hamper achievement of optimal esthetic results.
However, these limitations on the esthetic outcomes
can be overcome by rotation of the maxillomandibular
complex, also known as alteration or manipulation of
the occlusal plane. The alteration in occlusal plane angle
should not be considered as an attempt to change or treat
an abnormally high or low occlusal plane angle to a more
cephalometrically normal angle. The profound effect that
the occlusal plane may have on the relationship of the
apical bony base of the maxilla to the mandible as well
as on the anterior skull base is demonstrated in Fig 5-3.
The occlusal plane should therefore always be an import-

structures. The diagnostic information gained from the
preoperative clinical examination, 3D virtual imaging, study
casts, and radiographic evaluation must be carefully integrated to establish the appropriate surgical treatment plan.
When surgery to both jaws is planned and performed, the
final horizontal and vertical relationships among the anterior nasal spine (ANS), the maxillary incisor edge, and pogonion (Pog) are key determinants in the esthetic outcome.
The horizontal relationship among ANS, incisor edge, and
Pog is a consequence of the horizontal skeletal and dental
relationship (ie, Class I, II, or III). The vertical relationship
among these three key elements is simultaneously influenced by the vertical skeletal and dental pattern: vertical
maxillary excess or deficiency, vertical mandibular skeletal
excess or deficiency, and deep or open bite malocclusion.
The interrelationship of important soft tissue landmarks
such as the subnasale (Sn), upper lip position (Li, and its
relation to the maxillary central incisor), and Pog′ are directly
dependent on the position of the underlying skeletal and
dental structures (ANS, incisor edge, and Pog, respectively).

174

Fig 5-5 A triangle involving the PNS, ANS, and
Pog is used to plan the rotation of the maxillomandibular complex.

In some patients, the key hard tissue landmarks may
be well related; however, because of hyperdivergency or
hypodivergency of the occlusal and mandibular plane,
the patient may still have a convex or concave profile.
For example, in Fig 5-4, the vertical and anteroposterior
interrelationship of ANS, incisor edge, and Pog is identical
in each part, but the profiles differ substantially because
of the difference of the occlusal plane angle.
Alteration of the occlusal plane should be considered
only if conventional treatment planning does not yield
satisfactory results. It is extremely difficult to simply
“place” or “select” a new occlusal plane without selecting
a point around which to rotate the maxillomandibular
complex or alter the occlusal plane. It is easier and more
accurate, both in planning and in surgery, to rotate the
maxillomandibular complex around a preselected point.
The geometry of maxillomandibular complex rotation
and the expected effects are simplified and best illustrated
by constructing a triangle involving the posterior nasal
spine (PNS), ANS, and Pog (Fig 5-5). Any point on the

Fig 5-6 (a) Forty dry skulls were measured, and the anterior facial height was measured from ANS to Pog and compared with the maxillary length measured
from ANS to PNS. (b) The anterior facial height (ANS-Pog = 66.5 mm) is longer than the maxillary length (ANS-PNS = 47.6 mm). (c) Because the horizontal
leg (B1) is shorter than the vertical leg (A1) of the triangle, small changes (b1) will be accentuated (a1), resulting in a gearing effect.

triangle posterior or inferior to ANS may be chosen as the
rotation point. The location of the point and the direction
of rotation are dictated by the esthetic requirements of
each patient. Opposite esthetic results can be achieved
by either clockwise or counterclockwise rotation of the
maxillomandibular complex. In addition, the effects can
be enhanced by moving the point more anteriorly or posteriorly between ANS and PNS, or inferiorly or superiorly
between ANS and Pog. The desired position of the maxillary incisor, paranasal anatomy, and chin prominence will
help establish the point of rotation.
The cephalometric tracing (with the constructed maxillomandibular triangle) is used to investigate various soft
tissue and hard tissue changes that can be expected by
rotating the complex in a clockwise direction (increase
in occlusal plane angle) and counterclockwise direction
(decrease in occlusal plane angle). The method of developing a surgical visual treatment objective is discussed
in chapter 3.

Linear dimensions between ANS and PNS
In a study of 40 dry skulls, Reyneke found that the average
maxillary length (ANS to PNS—the horizontal leg of the
maxillomandibular triangle) was 47.6 mm and the average
facial height (ANS to Pog—the vertical leg of the maxillomandibular triangle) was 66.5 mm (Figs 5-6a and 5-6b) (see
Recommended Reading). The fact that the horizontal leg is
shorter than the vertical leg will enable the surgeon to use a
“gearing” effect when rotating the triangle. Small rotations

of the horizontal leg will result in larger changes at the vertical leg (Fig 5-6c). The anterior or posterior movement of
Pog (b1) is greater than the superior or inferior repositioning of the posterior maxilla (a1) because the height of the
lower third of the face (ANS to Pog [B1]) is greater than the
anteroposterior maxillary length (ANS to PNS [A1]). The
ratio of the movements may be expressed as the following:
a1 = A1
b1		 B1
The next section discusses varied dental, skeletal, and
soft tissue changes resulting from rotation of the maxillomandibular complex and its esthetic result compared with
that of conventional treatment planning, and clinical cases
in which this concept is used are demonstrated.
Geometry of the treatment design using the
constructed maxillomandibular triangle
Patients with Class III malocclusion who require
surgery on both jaws will need maxillary advancement
and mandibular setback, which will change the profile
from concave (low facial contour angle) to straight
(increased facial contour angle). This soft tissue effect
will be enhanced by clockwise rotation of the maxillomandibular triangle and limited by counterclockwise
rotation. The surgical correction of convex profiles will
often require superior repositioning of the maxilla and
mandibular advancement, resulting in a straighter profile.
The corrective effect will be enhanced by counterclockwise rotation and limited by clockwise rotation of the
maxillomandibular complex.

It should be kept in mind that the convexity or concavity of the facial profile should not be changed by means of
an advancement or reduction of the chin by genioplasty
in patients with good chin contour. Chin contour is more
important than chin projection (position of Pog), and
genioplasty is only indicated where chin contour needs
to be enhanced. The maxillomandibular triangle should
therefore be constructed once the ideal chin contour has
been established with Pog in its final position.

Rotation point at the zygomatic buttress
When using rotation points on the horizontal line posterior to the ANS, a vertical component is added to changes.

Rotation point at ANS
Clockwise rotation
Surgical superior repositioning of PNS as a result of clockwise rotation around a point at ANS will result in the
changes depicted in Tables 5-1 and 5-2 (Fig 5-7a).

Counterclockwise rotation
In counterclockwise rotation of the maxillomandibular
complex at the zygomatic buttress, the changes depicted
in Tables 5-1 and 5-2 can be expected (Fig 5-8b).

Counterclockwise rotation
Counterclockwise rotation around a point at ANS will
require downgrafting the posterior maxilla and will result
in the changes depicted in Tables 5-1 and 5-2 (Fig 5-7b).

Clockwise rotation
The hard and soft tissue changes shown in Tables 5-1 and
5-2 can be expected when the maxillomandibular complex
is rotated clockwise around a point at the zygomatic
buttress (Fig 5-8a).

Rotation point at PNS
Moving the rotation point more posteriorly will have a
more substantial vertical effect on the structures on the
vertical leg (incisor edge and Pog) of the triangle.
