Fig 5-10 Clockwise rotation of the maxillomandibular complex around the maxillary incisor tip.
This rotation (red arrows) will advance the ANS
(top black arrow), set Pog back (bottom black
arrow), and superiorly reposition the PNS. The
incisor will become slightly upright. Note that a
counterclockwise rotation with a selected rotation point inferior to the ANS is seldom indicated.

Clockwise rotation
This surgical design is often indicated for patients with
vertical maxillary deficiency, low mandibular and occlusal
plane angles, and short faces. The lower facial height will
increase, and the profile will become more convex (Fig
5-9a; see Tables 5-1 and 5-2).
Counterclockwise rotation
This surgical design will enhance the surgical advancement of the mandible and is indicated for patients with
vertical maxillary excess, very high occlusal and mandibular plane angles, increased facial convexity, and Class
II malocclusion. Ideal esthetic correction may require a
large mandibular advancement. However, it may not be
possible during the orthodontic preparation to create a
sufficient overjet to allow for the required mandibular
advancement. In these instances, counterclockwise rota-

178

tion of the maxillomandibular complex around a point on
the horizontal leg and as far as possible posteriorly should
be considered (Fig 5-9b).
Rotation at the incisor edge
Clockwise rotation
Clockwise rotation of the maxillomandibular complex
around the incisor edge will require the posterior maxilla
to be superiorly repositioned and advanced. This rotation
will result in the changes depicted in Tables 5-1 and 5-2
(Fig 5-10).
Counterclockwise rotation
Counterclockwise rotation would result in posterior repositioning of the maxilla, which is very rarely indicated,
technically difficult, and not recommended.

Indications for the rotation of the
maxillomandibular complex
A severely convex face, as demonstrated in Fig 5-11a, will
require vertical reduction of the maxilla combined with
mandibular and chin advancement. This procedure will
change the convex profile to a straight one. The corrective
measure can be enhanced by counterclockwise rotation
of the maxillomandibular complex and can be limited by
clockwise rotation. In Fig 5-11b, the substantial change of
the profile is demonstrated (note that the chin contour
has been maintained). The surgical design has eliminated
the need for an excessive advancement of the chin with
a genioplasty. The correction of Class III dentofacial
deformities will often require maxillary advancement and
mandibular setback, and in many instances, this skeletal
correction will result in a functional and stable occlusion.

b

It will also change a concave profile to a straighter one and
improve esthetics. However, to increase the convexity of
the profile or enhance the corrective change, the maxillomandibular complex should be rotated in a clockwise
direction. In patients requiring a less convex profile or
when the corrective change is limited, the maxillomandibular complex should be rotated in a counterclockwise
direction (Fig 5-12).
The surgeon is often required to improve the esthetics of
a patient who has a skeletal discrepancy and for whom the
occlusion has been orthodontically compromised (see case
1, Fig 5-14). In these instances, the principle of rotation of the
maxillomandibular complex should be considered. Figure
5-13 demonstrates the occlusion (compromised Class I) of
a situation like this. The first premolars were removed, the
maxillary incisors retracted, and the mandibular incisors
proclined as compromised treatment for skeletal vertical

Case 1
The patient in Fig 5-14 was a very unhappy 16-year-old
adolescent boy who had undergone 4 years of orthodontic
treatment. His dentition was severely compromised for his
skeletal vertical maxillary excess and mandibular anteroposterior deficiency, and his facial appearance was very compromised as well. The surgeon had two treatment options:
Option 1
• Extract mandibular first premolars
• Decompensate mandibular incisors
• Improve the angulation of the maxillary incisors
• Surgical superior reposition the maxilla
• Surgically advance the mandible
• Surgically advance the chin

180

8.5

e

Option 2
• Accept the current occlusion
• Rotate the maxillomandibular complex in a counterclockwise direction
• Use the zygomatic buttress as rotation point
• Advance the chin with a sliding genioplasty

After 4 years of orthodontic treatment, the patient was
very reluctant to have further orthodontic treatment, and the
author decided to choose the second treatment option. The
orthodontic appliances were removed 7 weeks after surgery.
The treatment results are illustrated in Figs 5-14d to 5-14f.
For patients requiring superior repositioning of the
maxilla for the correction of vertical maxillary excess
in combination with rotation of the maxillomandibular
complex, the maxilla is first superiorly repositioned on the
surgical visual treatment objective, and the maxillomandibular complex is then rotated (see Fig 5-13).

Fig 5-15 (a) A lingual view of the mandible in preparation for clockwise rotation of the distal segment (arrow i). A segment of bone is removed superior

In addition to the esthetic objectives, anatomical
considerations also influence the selection of the center
of rotation and the extent of change of the occlusal cant.
Excessive superior repositioning of the PNS (more than
5 to 6 mm) may compromise the nasal airway and is also
technically difficult. Where indicated, therefore, it may
be necessary to perform a horseshoe-shaped Le Fort I
osteotomy to separate the dentoalveolar portions of the
maxilla from the palate, which remains attached to the
nasal septum. The vertical dimension of the nasal airway
is thereby maintained, and the dentoalveolar segments are
free to be repositioned.
An excessive change in the occlusal plane angle
could affect the balance between the condylar guidance
of the articular tubercle (eminentia articularis) and the
cuspal angulations responsible for protrusive disclusion.
However, the interrelationship and possible effects of
changes require further research.

182

Clockwise rotation of the distal segment of the mandible may lead to segmental interference at the step of the
horizontal osteotomy on the medial side of the ramus. This
step may need to be contoured (Fig 5-15a) to allow free
movement of the segment. The anterior edge of the segment
often requires contouring as well (Fig 5-15b). The method
for developing a cephalometric prediction tracing involving
the rotation of the maxillomandibular complex is discussed
in chapter 3.
Clockwise rotation of the maxillomandibular complex
around a point posterior to the zygomatic buttress allows
vertical lengthening of the anterior facial height.

This case demonstrates clockwise rotation of the maxillomandibular complex around the ANS (Fig 5-16).

Fig 5-19 (a) Malocclusion of a child in mixed dentition: an anterior open bite and tendency to posterior crossbites. (b) The removable appliance with crib

Open Bite Deformities
Open bite dentofacial deformities are some of the most
challenging deformities facing surgeons and orthodontists. Diagnosis and formulation of a treatment plan can
be difficult because the etiologic factors can be challenging
to distinguish. There may be neuromuscular influences,
genetic growth factors, and habits. Furthermore, the longterm dental and skeletal stability following treatment are
concerns because neuromusculature and/or persistent
habits may continue to influence the repositioned jaws
and teeth.
The main etiologic factors that should be considered
are abnormal sucking habits (eg, thumbsucking), oropharyngeal and/or nasopharyngeal obstruction (eg, enlarged
adenoids), or vertical skeletal abnormalities (eg, vertical
maxillary excess or shortening of the mandibular ramus).
Researchers have developed two philosophies: (1) the
morphogenetic theory and (2) the adaptive theory. The
morphogenetic theory suggests that an anterior open bite
may be the result of aberrant genetic control of morphology via growth patterns, whereas the adaptive philosophy
postulates that a malformation develops secondary to
functional aberrations of the oropharyngeal and nasopharyngeal apparatus.

Abnormal sucking habits
Nonnutritive sucking is a normal developmental phenomenon that usually disappears with age. Persistence of the
habit beyond the age of 6 years is strongly associated with
the development of open bite malocclusion (ie, proclination of the incisors and narrowing of the maxillary arch),
although not all children who are thumbsuckers will
develop open bites. Control of the sucking habit may lead
to spontaneous correction of the malocclusion; however,
some patients may need additional orthodontic expansion of the maxillary dental arch (Fig 5-19). When patient
response to this treatment is poor, excessive vertical skeletal growth should be suspected (Fig 5-20).

Oropharyngeal or nasopharyngeal obstruction
There are several factors that may cause oropharyngeal or
nasopharyngeal obstruction, such as enlarged adenoids
and/or tonsils, allergic rhinitis, obstructive sleep apnea,
and deviated nasal septum. The neuromuscular response
to these factors has been shown to influence and alter
facial and dental structures. However, these morphologic
changes may show some recovery following removal of the
deleterious impact. Although not all patients respond in the
same way to these factors, the neuromuscular responses
usually include at least one if not all of the following:
• Altered mandibular position. The mandible rotates downward and backward in response to the etiologic factor.
• Altered tongue posture. The tongue moves superiorly
and anteriorly.
• Extended head posture. The cranium and maxilla rotate
upward.
There are several implications of these functional adaptations to nasal breathing. First, a change in the direction
of mandibular growth from horizontal to vertical results in
increased lower facial height. Second, superior and anterior positioning of the tongue has several dental effects,
including narrowing of the maxillary arch caused by the

187

5 | Diagnosis and Treatment of Complex Dentofacial Deformities

unopposed action of the buccinator muscles, retroclination
of the maxillary incisors caused by the unopposed actions of
the orbicularis oris muscle, and proclination of the mandibular incisors caused by increased tongue pressure. Not all
patients respond in the same way to diagnostic factors such
as nasopharyngeal obstruction, and in some cases, increased
resistance to nasal breathing may be unimportant. Some
patients with upper airway obstruction will respond with
increased chest activity to overcome the obstruction, and no
malocclusion may ensue. The altered head posture (extension or backward rotation) is an attempt to improve breathing. The change in head position has been associated with
changes in craniofacial morphology, including increased
facial height, mandibular anteroposterior deficiency, and
steep mandibular and occlusal plane angle.

Skeletal
Typically, patients with anterior open bite will have
increased lower facial height. An open bite dentofacial
deformity is basically a vertical condition, and an anterior open bite may develop either as a result of vertical
maxillary excess or shortening of the mandibular ramus.
Excessive vertical development of the maxilla will result
in backward and downward rotation of the mandible, leading to the development of an anterior open bite. Increased
vertical development of the maxilla has also been associated with several muscle weakness syndromes. It has been
reported that patients with long faces have biting forces
below normal; however, the relationship between decreased
biting forces, vertical maxillary excess, and anterior open
bite is not clear. Shortening of the mandibular ramus may
also result in clockwise rotation of the mandible and lead
to anterior open bite. A short ramus may be the result of
genetic growth predisposition or condylar resorption.
Tongue thrust swallowing and/or abnormal tongue activity during speech has been blamed for the development of
anterior open bite malocclusions and poor stability following
treatment in the past. Various methods to change swallowing patterns, such as speech therapy and use of a removable
appliance with a crib, have been implemented to control
the habit and ensure posttreatment stability. Contemporary
research has shown, however, that the tongue thrust swallowing pattern is a physiologic response to an anterior open
bite rather than the cause of it. The use of a crib in an adult
patient is of no value; however, correction of the open bite will
eliminate the need to thrust the tongue during swallowing.
An abnormally large tongue or true macroglossia should be
differentiated from pseudomacroglossia and then considered

188

Fig 5-21 Typical crenations on the lateral sides (arrows) of a large tongue
(macroglossia). Note that the relaxed tongue is covering the occlusal surfaces
of the mandibular teeth.

as an etiologic factor for open bite. Patients with open bite in
whom macroglossia is an etiologic factor will have bimaxillary dental protrusion, diastemas between the incisors, and
crenations on the lateral aspects of the tongue (Fig 5-21). An
abnormally large tongue does not adjust to the decreased oral
volume after surgical correction and plays an important role in
postoperative relapse (see case 5, Fig 5-22). However, a normalsized tongue with forward posturing does adjust to the smaller
volume after surgery. Pretreatment tongue thrust swallowing
disappears after correction of an open bite because the physiologic necessity for tongue thrust has been eliminated.
Case 5
A 14-year-old adolescent girl presented with a skeletal Class
III anterior open bite malocclusion (Figs 5-22a and 5-22b).
The significance of her large tongue was unfortunately not
considered in the diagnosis. Her orthodontic treatment in
preparation for orthognathic surgery started at the age of
16 years and consisted of leveling and alignment of her
dentition, retraction of her mandibular incisors, closing of
the interdental diastemas, and coordination of the dental
arches (Figs 5-22c and 5-22d). At the age of 18 years, her
mandible was set back by means of a bilateral sagittal split
osteotomy (BSSO), and the orthodontic appliances were
removed 4 months after surgery (Fig 5-22e). Over a period of
6 years, however, there was severe relapse of the results (Figs
5-22f to 5-22h). Figure 5-22i illustrates the dental, skeletal, and
soft tissue components of the relapse 6 years after treatment.
The patient returned 15 years after the initial surgery,
requesting correction of her malocclusion. The patient’s
macroglossia was certainly a major etiologic contributor to
her relapse. The surgeon therefore decided to correct the
open bite by performing a Le Fort I osteotomy, which would
create more space for the large tongue; however, it was also
planned to surgically reduce the size of the tongue. The cephalometric analysis and the orthodontic and surgical visual
treatment objectives for the corrective treatment are illustrated in Figs 5-22j to 5-22l. The treatment plan consisted of

Open Bite Deformities

Open bite malocclusion in growing individuals
In young, growing individuals, the major causes of anterior
open bite are prolonged thumbsucking (beyond the age of
6 years) and environmental influences. The open bite as a
result of thumbsucking is usually limited to the anterior
region with a narrow maxillary dental arch, posterior crossbites, and relatively normal facial proportions. The most
important corrective measure for these patients is to stop
the habit. The posterior crossbites in the mixed dentition
can be corrected by maxillary expansion using a removable
appliance. The appliance can also assist in habit control
with the placement of a crib (see Fig 5-19). If the open bite
persists by the time the patient reaches adolescence, with
poor response to habit control and maxillary expansion,
skeletal factors should be considered.
Mouth breathing as a result of oropharyngeal and
nasopharyngeal obstruction of the posterior airway
space should always be considered in young patients as
a possible etiologic factor in the development of an anterior open bite. Several studies have shown that removal
of the cause of the obstruction to normal breathing (eg,
enlarged adenoids or tonsils, allergic rhinitis) may decrease
the open bite. However, it is important to differentiate
between mouth breathing as a result of nasopharyngeal
obstruction and mouth breathing as a result of vertical
maxillary excess and lip incompetence.
A poor response following removal of an oropharyngeal
or nasopharyngeal obstruction is indicative of excessive
vertical skeletal growth.

Open bite malocclusion in nongrowing individuals
Orthodontic correction of anterior open bite
Orthodontic correction of skeletal anterior open bite often
leads to poor stability and compromised esthetics. However,
some patients with anterior open bites can be successfully
treated with orthodontics. Orthodontic correction of anterior open bite without surgery usually focuses on three goals:
(1) extrusion of maxillary and mandibular incisors, (2) intrusion of posterior teeth, and (3) expansion of the maxillary
dental arch. This orthodontic treatment requires the almost
exclusive use of vertical mechanics. Extrusion of the incisors
may be accomplished in three ways: (1) vertical elastics in
the anterior region; (2) a continuous archwire from molar
to molar to level the excessive curve of Spee in the maxillary
arch; and (3) a continuous archwire from molar to molar
to level a reverse curve of Spee in the mandibular arch.
Mechanics to intrude the molars may include temporary

anchorage devices, bite blocks with repelling magnets, and
high-pull headgear and anterior box elastics. Expansion of
the maxillary arch in adult patients with skeletal maxillary
transverse deficiency usually involves tipping of the molars
and questionable stability. For patients in whom the anterior
open bite is associated with incisor protrusion (increased
incisor angulation), correction of the incisor angulation
by tipping the incisors has a relative extrusion effect, thus
closing the bite.
Few studies reporting on the orthodontic correction of
anterior open bite have addressed pretreatment esthetics
and the facial esthetic outcomes. Regardless of the specific
mechanics used to achieve tooth movement, stability is
often unpredictable and frequently results in compromised
esthetics. When initial orthodontic attempts to close an
anterior open bite have failed and the patient is referred
for surgical correction, the possible instability of the previous orthodontic treatment should be carefully assessed.
It would be wise to compare the patient’s pretreatment
records with the current dental situation. If any unstable
tooth movements are suspected, it is recommended that
the archwires be segmentalized and the teeth be allowed
to relapse before continuation of any further orthodontic
or surgical treatment.
Combined surgical and orthodontic correction
of anterior open bite
Anterior open bite secondary to vertical maxillary
excess: Le Fort I maxillary osteotomy with or without
mandibular surgery
The common but variable clinical, dental, and cephalometric features of patients with anterior open bite as a result
of vertical maxillary excess are shown and compared with
the features of those with open bite as a result of short
mandibular rami later in the chapter (see Table 5-4).
Presurgical orthodontics. Orthodontic correction of vertical,
transverse, or anteroposterior skeletal conditions should be
avoided. Orthodontic tooth movements for the correction
of these problems have questionable long-term stability,
and relapse might occur after the surgery is completed. An
important orthodontic decision to be made is whether the
maxillary arch should be aligned in one segment or multiple
segments. When a natural step is present in the maxillary
arch or surgical expansion is planned, the maxillary arch
should be aligned in segments. The use of mechanics to close
the bite or expand the posterior teeth beyond the bony base
should be avoided. This does not mean that teeth within a
segment should not be leveled; correction of incisor angu-

Fig 5-23 The five key factors to be considered when planning
correction of an anterior open bite: (1) the amount of superior
repositioning of the anterior maxilla to achieve the ideal maxillary
incisor–upper lip relationship; (2) the amount of superior repositioning of the posterior maxilla to allow for mandibular autorotation and closure of the anterior open bite; (3) any anteroposterior
discrepancy following mandibular autorotation and whether it can
be corrected by maxillary surgery (care should be taken not to

indicates the planned vertical location of the mandibular incisor
after autorotation; and (5) any maxillary transverse discrepancies
to be addressed.

lations may change their height without extrusion. Opening
the bite before surgery improves stability because relapse
following intrusion will serve to close the bite further after
surgery. The need for extraction is indicated by the amount
of crowding and the tooth movements required to place the
teeth at the correct angulations and in the central trough of
bone. If the anterior maxillary segment contains only the
four incisors, their angulations can be altered surgically;
however, when the canines are included in the segment,
surgical correction of the angulation will lift the canines
out of the occlusion. Care should be taken to coordinate
the arch forms of the segments with the mandibular arch
form and to deviate the tooth roots adjacent to the intended
interdental osteotomy sites. The mandible will serve as the
template for the maxilla, and the mandibular arch should
be leveled and the arch form aligned to allow for orthodontic (one-piece maxilla) or surgical (segmental maxillary
surgery) coordination.
Surgical treatment. The surgical correction of an anterior
open bite as a result of vertical maxillary excess will require
superior repositioning of the maxilla by means of a Le Fort I
osteotomy. There are five important factors that should be
considered during the planning of the treatment (Fig 5-23):
1. How far should the anterior maxilla be superiorly repositioned?
In most patients, the maxillary incisors require superior
repositioning. Whereas in some patients the incisor height
should be maintained, in others, inferior repositioning of
the anterior maxilla may be required. The final vertical position of the maxillary incisors will be dictated by the incisorlip relationship (ideal amount of incisor exposure under

192

the upper lip). The final anteroposterior position will be
dictated by the required amount of upper lip support. The
final vertical and anteroposterior positions of the maxillary
incisors are the keys to treatment planning.
2. How far should the posterior maxilla be superiorly repositioned? After superior repositioning of the maxilla, the
mandible will autorotate to close the anterior open bite.
The vertical position of the maxillary molars is dictated
by the height of the mandibular molars (mandibular
occlusal plane) after mandibular autorotation.
3. What will be the location of the mandible after autorotation? Vertical change of the maxilla will always have
anteroposterior consequences as a result of rotation of
the mandible around the condyle. Following superior
repositioning of the maxilla, the mandible will rotate
counterclockwise around a point at the condyle. The
bite will close anteriorly, and the occlusal relationship
will now be either Class I, II, or III. The incisor relationship may be ideal, or an increased overjet or anterior
crossbite may be present.
4. Can the maxilla compensate for the secondary anteroposterior discrepancy that may have developed as a result of
the mandibular autorotation (Table 5-3)? Patients with
Class II malocclusion and vertical maxillary excess will
end up with Class I malocclusion as a result of the anterior rotation of the mandible. These patients will not
require mandibular surgery; however, genioplasty may
be indicated to improve the chin contour. The mandible of a patient with a Class I malocclusion will rotate
forward into a Class III malocclusion following superior
repositioning of the maxilla. Based on esthetic requirements, the clinician must decide whether the Class III

relationship should be corrected by mandibular setback
or maxillary advancement. Patients with Class III anterior open bite and vertical maxillary excess will end
up with Class III malocclusion with increased severity
following vertical correction and mandibular autorotation. A good esthetic outcome in these patients will
require advancement and superior repositioning of
the maxilla in conjunction with a mandibular setback
procedure. Patients who have severe Class II anterior
open bite malocclusion will still end up with a Class
II malocclusion after autorotation. The correction of
the Class II relationship should not be done by maxillary setback because this procedure will often result in
poor midface esthetics. Instead, the mandible should be
advanced and the maxilla both superiorly repositioned
and slightly advanced. The slight advancement has the
added technical advantage that the posterior maxilla
is moved away from the pterygoid plates, which allows
for easy and adequate superior repositioning of the
posterior maxilla. In all of these treatment scenarios,
the chin contour and position should be evaluated to
enhance the esthetic outcome. When considering a
genioplasty procedure, two important aspects should
be kept in mind: (1) genioplasty is not a substitute for
mandibular surgery, and (2) the shape and contour of
the chin is more important than the chin position (the
anteroposterior position of Pog).
5. How should transverse occlusal discrepancies be corrected?
An individual with an anterior open bite and skeletal
vertical maxillary excess often has a narrow maxillary
dental arch and bilateral crossbites. Any transverse
occlusal problems should be corrected by orthodontic

expansion, surgically assisted orthodontic expansion,
or surgical expansion. Skeletal transverse maxillary
deficiencies should be corrected by either surgically
assisted orthodontic expansion or surgical expansion, whereas pure dental narrowing of the maxillary
arch should be corrected by orthodontic expansion.
Surgically assisted expansion of the maxillary arch is
indicated in patients who would not need any other
maxillary procedure, ie, superior repositioning of the
maxilla. Surgical expansion by segmental surgery is
recommended for patients in whom expansion in addition to surgical superior repositioning of the maxilla is
required. Transverse relapse is one of the most common
postsurgical complications and inevitably leads to
recurrence of the anterior open bite. The problem often
stems from poor preoperative diagnosis, inappropriate
presurgical orthodontics, poor surgical management,
and poor postoperative orthodontic control. When the
crossbite is skeletal in nature, compensatory orthodontic dental expansion, headgear, archwires, and
through-the-bite elastics should be avoided. These
orthodontic methods used on a skeletal crossbite carry
a high potential for relapse and may “build” relapse into
the surgical result. If the patient was not seen by the
surgeon before commencement of treatment, it may
be wise to compare pretreatment records with current
records before surgery is performed. This knowledge
will enable the clinician to establish if any unstable
orthodontic tooth movement was performed before
surgery. Presurgical orthodontic tipping of the molars
may leave the buccal cusps hanging above the occlusal
plane and will cause additional surgical complications.

Fig 5-24 (a) The molars have been uprighted (arrows) and are in the middle trough of bone. (b) The intermolar distance has

expanded and tipped buccally (arrows) with the lingual cusps “hanging.” (d) A 5-mm intermolar expansion will now require a
surgical expansion of 10.5 mm.

Surgical palatal expansion in these patients will involve
expansion of the bony base as well as uprighting of the
molars. The surgical dental uprighting will add to the
amount of skeletal expansion, increasing the potential
for relapse (Fig 5-24). Transverse stability following surgical segmental expansion can be enhanced by placing a
bone graft in the palatal defect and by using a splint during
surgery that is maintained in position for at least 6 weeks
afterward. Postoperative stability can be further improved
by appropriate postoperative orthodontics. Postoperative
orthodontic control should involve placement of a palatal bar, a strong archwire, or both to support the palatal
expansion after removal of the splint.
Open bite secondary to short mandibular ramus with
a normal condyle: Mandibular surgery
The clinical features of patients with anterior open bite as
a result of a short mandibular ramus differ from patients
with vertical maxillary excess (Table 5-4). When considering the difference in clinical features in Table 5-4, the
clinician should keep in mind that some patients may have
developed an open bite as a result of both vertical maxillary
excess and short mandibular ramus.

194

Surgical orthodontics. In general, the same presurgical
orthodontic treatment principles as with open bite as a
result of vertical maxillary excess should be followed.
Surgical treatment. The anatomical abnormality, esthetic
features, and functional correction in these patients
demand a different surgical approach. Consideration
should be given to closure of the bite with mandibular
surgery. This surgical approach will require a counterclockwise rotation of the mandible at the posterior teeth.
Historically, this surgical movement of the mandible has
been considered unstable. An important factor causing
the instability and tendency to relapse is thought to be
the increase in posterior mandibular height as a result of
the downward rotation of the posterior part of the distal
segment and the subsequent stretching of the pterygomandibular sling (Figs 5-25a and 5-25b). When the distal
segment of the mandible is rotated counterclockwise
during surgery, two muscle groups are stretched: the
masseter and medial pterygoid muscles and the suprahyoid muscles. The suprahyoid muscles are stretched even
further when the mandible is rotated counterclockwise
and advanced at the same time. The original sagittal split

technique as described by Obwegeser and Trauner in 1955
was further modified several times, first by Hunsuck (1968)
and later by Epker (1977). According to the modification,
the mandible is split just posterior to the inferior alveolar
foramen. By using this technique, counterclockwise rotation of the distal segment would not increase the posterior
mandibular height and therefore not interfere with the
pterygomassetric sling (Figs 5-25c and 5-25d). As suggested
in chapter 6, the medial pterygoid muscle and stylomandibular ligament should be dissected off the medial side
of the mandibular angle to ensure free positioning of the
proximal segment. The author recommends the correction
of an anterior open bite secondary to a short mandibular
ramus with a healthy TMJ to be corrected with mandibular
surgery (Fig 5-26).

Open bite secondary to short mandibular ramus with
an abnormal condyle
The morphology and position of the condyle in the glenoid
fossa is altered by any process of bone resorption of the
condyle. Resorption of the condyle and subsequent shortening of the mandibular rami will lead to the development
of a Class II anterior open bite malocclusion, a convex
profile, and mandibular deficiency. Before considering
correction of an anterior open bite resulting from condylar resorption, the clinician should differentiate between
idiopathic condylar resorption, degenerative joint disease,
osteoarthritis, osteonecrosis, traumatic injuries, and rheumatic arthritis with destruction of the condyle. Systemic
connective tissue and autoimmune disease also can cause
resorption of the condyle.

Fig 5-25 (a) Medial view of the mandible. The osteotomy design of the sagittal split according to Obwegeser and Trauner in 1955 (black line). Idiopathic condylar resorption (condylysis). This type of
condylar resorption may occur in any patient population;
however, it is one of the most common TMJ conditions
affecting teenage girls. Most of these patients are aged 10
to 16 years and usually have high mandibular plane angles.
These patients gradually develop an anterior open bite without pain or hypomobility. The process is usually self-limiting
and may last from 6 months to 2 years. Although the specific
cause of idiopathic condylar resorption is unknown, the fact
that there is a strong predilection for teenage girls in their
pubertal growth phase suggests that hormonal mediation
may play a role. It is hypothesized that through estrogen
mediation and excessive joint loading (eg, parafunction,
orthodontic treatment, orthognathic surgery, and trauma),
resorption is induced in the subcondylar bone in all three
planes of space. Although this condition usually occurs bilaterally, it may occur in only one condyle or at a more rapid
rate in one condyle than in the other.

196

There are three important aspects to consider when
planning the correction of the existing deformity: (1)
Ensure that the resorptive process has become inactive.
Serial cephalometric radiographs are a valuable diagnostic
method; however, an alternative method such as radioisotope analysis can be used to detect the presence of
resorptive activity in the condyle. (2) Evaluate the articular disc for pathology or displacement. Some surgeons
prefer to perform disc repositioning and placement of a
Mitek anchor (Johnson & Johnson), when indicated, at
the same time as the orthognathic correction. (3) Treat
the deformity in such a way that the loads on the condyles
are not increased (eg, avoid long Class III elastics during
orthodontic treatment).
Osteoarthrosis. Osteoarthrosis of the TMJ is a slow,
progressive, degenerative disease that alters the position,
morphology, and physiology of all joint structures. It may

Open Bite Deformities

Fig 5-26 (a) Profile view of a patient with mandibular anteroposterior deficiency. ( b) Profile

occur bilaterally or unilaterally, in which instance the
mandible will deviate to the affected side. Patients experience chronic pain, clicking, popping, crepitus, myofascial pain, and progressive changes in occlusion and facial
appearance. It may be possible to obtain temporary relief
of symptoms by conservative partial reconstruction of the
joint and orthognathic surgery in some patients whose
symptoms have not been present for a long time (less
than 4 years). However, for patients who have endured
the disease longer and whose condyles are significantly
destroyed and articular discs not salvageable, replacement of the joint with an artificial prosthesis would be the
procedure of choice.
Treatment planning for combined orthognathic surgery
and total joint replacement does not differ from conventional treatment planning. However, there is a limited
amount of mandibular advancement that can be obtained
with a joint prosthesis. The advancement is limited to an

amount of 7 to 8 mm of contact between the ramus and
the implant.
Anterior open bite dentofacial deformities are basically vertical problems that develop as a result of excessive vertical growth of the maxilla, vertical deficiency of
the mandibular ramus, or both. Although concomitant
anteroposterior deformities may also be present in these
patients, secondary anteroposterior problems are often
present as a result of clockwise rotation of the mandible.
The clinician should differentiate between primary and
secondary anteroposterior problems, which must be taken
into account during treatment planning. Transverse problems, as a result of a narrow maxillary dental arch, should
be treated and stability carefully considered because transverse occlusal stability ensures vertical occlusal stability.
Figure 5-27 is an algorithm that summarizes the suggested
principles of surgical orthodontic correction of anterior
open bite dentofacial deformities.

General characteristics of Class I open bite
Clinical characteristics
• Vertical maxillary excess, more posterior than anterior
• Mandible rotated clockwise (backward)
• An increased interlabial gap
• Often, increased exposure of maxillary incisors under
the upper lip
• Often, transverse maxillary deficiency
• Possibly a skeletal Class III malocclusion rotated to a
Class I malocclusion

Orthodontic preparation
Two decisions must be made in orthodontic preparation.
The first is to decide between continuous archwire (singlepiece) and segmental arch (multi-piece) mechanics. When
a dual occlusal plane exists, segmental arch orthodontics
is indicated to avoid possible orthodontic extrusion of
anterior teeth, which may lead to unpredictable stability

198

(Fig 5-28). Conversely, when a single occlusal plane exists,
continuous archwire mechanics is preferred (Fig 5-29).
The second decision is extraction versus nonextraction. The decision to extract teeth is influenced by
two factors: (1) crowding and (2) the anteroposterior
position of the mandibular incisors. When no mandibular
surgery is contemplated (according to the orthodontic and surgical prediction tracings), the final anteroposterior position of the maxilla will be dictated by the
anteroposterior position of the mandibular incisors after
autorotation of the mandible.
Surgical solutions
Following are surgical solutions to Class I open bites (Fig 5-30):
• Superior repositioning of the maxilla (using segmental or single-piece mechanics): When a preexisting
dual occlusal plane is present, segmental orthodontic
alignment followed by segmental surgical correction
is indicated.

in patients with a high occlusal plane angle). Patients

with facial features indicative of maxillary anteroposterior deficiency (large nose, convex nasal dorsum, deficient paranasal anatomy, lack of supratip break, and poor

experience unesthetic changes with isolated superior and
anterior repositioning of the maxilla. For these patients,
simultaneous mandibular setback is necessary to avoid
undesirable maxillary advancement. Finally, the facial
esthetics of a small percentage of patients may necessitate more maxillary advancement than is dictated by
the mandible after autorotation. For these patients, the
clinician should consider additional advancement of
the maxilla, which will require simultaneous surgical
advancement of the mandible.
The anteroposterior position of the chin is not a
primary determining factor in the patients previously
discussed. In most of these patients, excessive or deficient chin projection can be corrected by an appropriate

200

genioplasty; however, the shape of the chin and the depth
of the labiomental fold should be considered.
In summary, the surgical solutions may include the
following:
• Maxillary superior repositioning with or without genioplasty
• Maxillary superior repositioning with mandibular
setback, with or without genioplasty
• Maxillary superior repositioning with mandibular
advancement, with or without genioplasty
• Possible maxillary superior repositioning in a single
piece (more posterior than anterior, as indicated by
the mandibular occlusal plane after autorotation) or
in segments, allowing differential superior repositioning of the anterior and posterior segments and simultaneous expansion of the buccal segments (again, as
dictated by the occlusal plane of the mandible and the
mandibular dental arch form)

Orthodontic preparation
1. Single-piece Le Fort I osteotomy
• Level, align, and coordinate the maxillary arch, and
align the mandibular arch.
• Do not attempt to close the open bite by expansion of
the buccal segments, extrusion of the anterior teeth,
or intrusion of the posterior teeth.
• Expand the buccal segments of teeth only where the
teeth are palatally inclined in relation to the basal bone.
2. Segmental Le Fort I osteotomy
• Level and align the maxillary dental arch in segments.
• Align the segments to be compatible with the mandibular arch.
• Deviate the roots at the intended interdental osteotomy areas.
• Do not attempt to expand or close bites orthodontically.
• Level and align the mandibular arch.

condyle. The forward vector may be sufficient to allow only

• Increased interlabial gap
• Mandibular anteroposterior deficiency

Presurgical orthodontics
Maxillary arch (Fig 5-34e)
• Alignment of the maxillary arch in three segments, from
the right second premolar to the right second molar,
the right canine to the left canine, and the left second
premolar to the left second molar
• Extraction of both first premolars at the time of surgery
to allow ideal surgical positioning of the anterior maxillary segment
• Deviation of the roots of both canines and both second
premolars to allow surgical closure of the extraction
spaces of both first premolars

Mandibular arch (see Fig 5-34e)
• Extraction of the left mandibular first molar
• Closure of the space where the left first molar was
extracted
• Leveling and alignment of the arch

Surgical treatment
Both maxillary first premolars were extracted (Figs
5-34f and 5-34g). A three-piece Le Fort I osteotomy was
performed with the interdental osteotomy through the
extraction sites of the maxillary first premolars. Removal
of bone in the extraction spaces allowed surgical closure
of the spaces, and a palatal osteotomy allowed expansion
of the posterior segments (see Figs 5-34f and 5-34g). The
anterior maxillary segment was repositioned; this was
critical for two reasons: (1) the vertical upper lip–maxillary incisor relationship had to be maintained, and (2) the
segment could not be retracted excessively because that
would undermine lip support and increase the labiomental
angle. The posterior segment of the maxilla was superiorly
repositioned to allow the mandible to autorotate, and the
mandible was advanced with a bilateral sagittal split osteotomy (see Figs 5-34f and 5-34g).
Postsurgical orthodontics
Postsurgical orthodontics entailed final closure of
extraction spaces, finalization of the occlusion, and retainer
wear. Closure of the extraction space of the mandibular
left first molar prolonged the orthodontic preparation, and
surgery was performed 25 months after commencement
of orthodontic treatment. The orthodontic bands were
removed 9 months after surgery. The 2-year postsurgical
results are illustrated in Figs 5-34h to 5-34j.

General characteristics of Class III open bite
Clinical characteristics
• Mandibular anteroposterior excess (magnitude of the
anteroposterior discrepancy masked by backward rotation of the mandible)
• Maxillary vertical excess (more posterior than anterior)
• Transverse maxillary deficiency
• Posterior crossbites
• Class III malocclusion
• Often, a reverse curve of Spee
• Increased mandibular plane angle
• Clockwise rotation of the mandible improves the Class
III relationship, and vertical correction makes it worse
Orthodontic preparation
• Remove any existing dental compensation.
• Where segmental surgery is contemplated, align the
maxillary arch in segments, and deviate roots at interdental osteotomy areas.
• Level and align the mandibular arch.

1

Fig 5-35 (a) (1) The maxillary incisor–upper lip
relationship is ideal and should be maintained anteroposteriorly and vertically. (2) Autorotation of
the mandible. (3) Forward rotation of the ANS.
(4) Superior repositioning of the posterior maxilla.
(5) Final occlusal plane determined by the mandibular occlusal plane after rotation. (b) (1) Ideal
maxillary incisor–upper lip relationship. (2) The
mandible has autorotated. (3) Occlusion is more
Class III with an anterior crossbite. Options include
maxillary advancement (4), mandibular setback
(5), both options 4 and 5, and/or genioplasty to
improve the chin shape (6).

Surgical solutions
Following are surgical solutions to Class III open bite (Fig 5-35):
• Single-piece Le Fort I osteotomy with superior repositioning of the maxilla (more posterior than anterior)
to achieve the ideal maxillary incisor–upper lip relationship
• Segmental surgery with differential superior repositioning of the maxilla (the posterior segments more
superiorly than the anterior segment)
• Expansion of the buccal segments
• Mandibular setback
• Genioplasty
Comments on Class III open bite
Inferior repositioning of the anterior maxilla and superior
repositioning of the posterior maxilla are often indicated.
The anterior maxilla often may be vertically deficient in
patients with Class III open bite, and the maxillary tooth–
lip relationship is an important consideration in planning
the final vertical position of the maxilla.

Open Bite Deformities

The mandible will autorotate, and the Class III occlusal
relationship will worsen.
The anteroposterior discrepancy can be corrected by
advancement of the maxilla, mandibular setback, both
maxillary advancement and mandibular setback, and/
or genioplasty. The treatment of choice depends on the
esthetic requirements of the patient.
The cause of the open bite should be determined before
treatment planning. Open bites may develop because of
deficient eruption of maxillary incisors (anterior), deficient
eruption of mandibular incisors (anterior), deficient eruption of maxillary and/or mandibular molars (posterior), or
excessive vertical development of the posterior maxilla.
The key element of the analysis is evaluation of the vertical relationship of the maxillary incisors to the relaxed upper
lip (keep lip length in mind). Deficient eruption of mandibular incisors with a reverse curve of Spee is often caused by
an abnormal size and resting posture of the tongue.
Posterior open bites can develop in three ways:
1. Excessive vertical development on one side, often associated with facial asymmetry (eg, unilateral condylar
hyperplasia)
2. Deficient eruption of teeth on one side (eg, impacted
teeth) with normal symmetry
3. Bilateral deficient eruption of posterior teeth (eg,
cleidocranial dysostosis or large tongue) with normal
symmetry
Case 8
This case demonstrates the correction of a Class III anterior open bite. A 19-year-old man was referred to the orthodontist by his general practitioner for the correction of
an open bite.
Main complaint
The patient said he was unable to bite properly, and the
flat appearance of his midface bothered him.
Medical history
The patient had asthma. He had contracted hepatitis A at
age 15 years and recovered satisfactorily.
Clinical examination
1. Soft tissue
• Frontal view (Fig 5-36a)
– Deficient paranasal areas
– Increased interlabial distance
– Excessive lower lip vermilion exposure

• Profile view (Fig 5-36b)
– Concave profile
– Midface deficiency
– Everted lower lip
– Increased interlabial distance
– Deficient chin
2. Dental (Fig 5-36c)
– Class III malocclusion
– Anterior open bite
– Slightly compensated mandibular incisors
3. Skeletal (Fig 5-36d)
– Maxillary anteroposterior deficiency
– Mandibular anteroposterior excess
– Posterior maxillary vertical excess
– Microgenia
4. Radiographic
• Panoramic radiograph
– Impacted maxillary third molars and partially
erupted mandibular third molars
• Cephalometric radiograph (see Fig 5-36d)
– Class III malocclusion
– Class III skeletal relationship
– Maxillary anteroposterior deficiency
– Anterior open bite
– Slightly lingually inclined mandibular incisors
– Lack of dental support for the upper lip
– Everted lower lip
– Mandibular anteroposterior excess
– Flat chin
Problem list
• Class III anterior open bite malocclusion
• Maxillary anteroposterior excess
• Posterior maxillary vertical excess
• Incisor compensation

Surgical treatment
A Le Fort I maxillary osteotomy was used to advance the
maxilla by 3 mm. At the same time, the maxilla was superiorly repositioned 3 mm anteriorly (to establish the ideal
tooth-lip relationship) and 6 mm posteriorly (to allow
autorotation of the mandible; see Fig 5-36f).
Because autorotation of the mandible worsened the
Class III relationship, the mandible was set back 8 mm
with a BSSO. Finally, a 4-mm advancement genioplasty
was performed. The advancement of the chin not only
enhances the chin shape but also maintains the chin-throat
length after mandibular setback.
Postsurgical orthodontics
Postsurgical orthodontic treatment involved finalization of
the occlusion and retention. The presurgical orthodontic
preparation took 8 months, and the orthodontic bands
were removed 3 months after surgery. Figures 5-36k to
5-36n illustrate the treatment result after a total treatment
time of 11 months.

Maxillary expansion
The transverse dimension of the maxilla can be increased
in four ways: (1) orthodontically through dental tipping;
(2) via rapid palatal orthodontic expansion in growing
patients; (3) through surgically assisted orthodontic palatal
expansion in nongrowing patients; and (4) through surgical
expansion by means of segmental surgery. When planning
for the correction of transverse maxillary deficiency, the
strengths and weaknesses of each applicable option should
be understood and considered. The flowchart in Fig 5-37
summarizes the treatment options for transverse arch
discrepancies.
Maxillary transverse deficiency usually results in posterior crossbites and has an incidence among the general
population of approximately 9.4%. Because the relative
transverse dimension between the maxilla and mandible
is also influenced by their anteroposterior relationship,
Jacobs et al (1980) divided transverse discrepancies into
relative or absolute posterior crossbites. The determination
is made when the patient’s dental cast is held in central

Fig 5-37 This flowchart simplifies the decision-making process in relation to orthodontic dental expansion, rapid maxillary expansion (in adolescents),
surgically assisted expansion, and surgical expansion of the maxilla by means of segmental maxillary surgery. It is clear that orthodontic expansion should be
reserved when only dental expansion is required. Surgically assisted expansion is indicated when no orthognathic surgery is planned or when severe crowding
makes presurgical orthodontic preparation for orthognathic surgery impossible. Existing transverse maxillary deficiency can in most instances be corrected
by segmental expansion at the time of definitive orthognathic surgery.

relation and posterior crossbite is evident. The casts are
then moved to position the dental arches into a Class I
canine relationship. If the crossbite is corrected with the
anteroposterior alignment of the arches, it is considered a
relative transverse discrepancy. If the crossbite still exists
following the movement, it is considered an absolute transverse discrepancy (Fig 5-38).
An absolute posterior crossbite may occur bilaterally or
unilaterally and is often associated with a Class II anterior
open bite dentofacial deformity. However, absolute crossbites may also occur in Class I and Class III malocclusions.
The etiology of posterior crossbites may be congenital,
traumatic, iatrogenic, or developmental. The restricted

210

transverse growth caused by surgical intervention in
patients with cleft lip and palate is an example of iatrogenic origin, whereas long-term thumbsucking is a good
example of developmental etiology.
The typical clinical findings in a patient with a posterior
crossbite are a narrow maxillary dental arch (often with an
hourglass-shaped arch form), a high palatal vault, dental
crowding often with palatally inclined posterior teeth, an
anterior open bite, and a Class II malocclusion. A dental
crossbite involving only one or two teeth should be treated
orthodontically. However, a crossbite involving more than
two teeth is likely skeletal in nature, and a surgical orthodontic approach should be considered.

Open Bite Deformities

Fig 5-38 Assessment of posterior crossbites on the
dental casts. (a) In patients with posterior crossbites,
the handheld dental casts are repositioned so that
the canines are in a Class I relationship. If a crossbite
still exists, it should be considered an absolute crossbite and must be addressed during anteroposterior
correction of the occlusion. (b) However, if the
crossbites are corrected by moving the casts (arrow),
the crossbites are relative and will be rectified automatically during correction of anteroposterior
discrepancies.

a

Orthodontic expansion
The treatment usually involves dental tipping and is
indicated when the skeletal base width is adequate. The
required transverse arch dimension can be achieved
by moving the teeth buccally using orthodontic forces;
however, stability is doubtful after orthodontic tipping of
the posterior teeth beyond their bony base. Dental tipping
leads to “hanging” of the palatal cusps of the posterior
teeth below the occlusal plane and will complicate subsequent surgical expansion. The increased angulation of the
molars will require additional surgical expansion of the
palate (see Fig 2-24). This action will not only jeopardize
the skeletal stability but will also build orthodontic dental
relapse into the surgical result.
Rapid palatal expansion
Growth of the maxilla takes place at the suture margins by
deposition of new bone from the adjacent cellular layer. The
principle of orthopedic expansion of the palate relies on
the physiology of normal active growth and elasticity of the
new bone at the suture lines. Cellular activity at the suture
lines decreases with age, and a wide age range—between
16 and 35 years—before completion of the synostosis has
been reported. However, it is generally accepted that rapid
palatal expansion without surgical assistance is clinically
successful only until the age of 14 to 16 years. As much as
30% to 50% of the gain in the transverse dimension with
this method can be lost as a result of relapse, which proves
that most of the width is obtained by dental tipping and
not skeletal expansion. Orthopedic expansion of the maxilla
beyond the age of 16 years has certain disadvantages, including severe pain, pressure necrosis of the palatal soft tissue,
dental tipping with no skeletal change, and uncontrolled
relapse.

b

Surgically assisted palatal expansion
This treatment option should be considered in the following
situations: (1) for the creation of arch length in patients with
severe crowding and when extractions are not an option
(eg, premolars already removed); (2) for patients in whom
unilateral maxillary expansion is required; (3) where maxillary expansion is the only surgery indicated; and (4) for
any patient in whom orthodontic expansion has failed and
suture resistance must be overcome. The surgical technique involves bilateral osteotomies of the lateral walls of
the maxilla carried from the piriform apertures past the
zygomatic buttresses. The pterygomaxillary junctures are
separated, and the palatal osteotomy is carried forward from
the PNS to just posterior to the incisive canal. Two paramedian osteotomies may be performed in patients who require
large expansions; this design also prevents unfavorable nasal
septum deviation. The distractor may be tooth-borne or
bone-borne, and the device should remain in place for at
least 8 to 10 weeks following completion of distraction.
Relapse occurs following distraction with both appliances;
however, the bone-borne appliances prove to be more stable.
Surgical expansion
Surgical expansion can be accomplished by either posterior
segmental osteotomies or a Le Fort I osteotomy, segmenting the maxilla in the downfractured position. When a
large amount of expansion is required, bilateral palatal
osteotomies should be performed. The osteotomy should
be made just lateral to the nasal septum where the palatal
bone is thin and the mucosa thick. Soft tissue release may
be helpful for large expansion and should be performed off
the osteotomy line. Care should also be taken to ensure
that the nasal mucosa is intact when performing soft tissue
release. Perforation of the nasal mucosa as well as the palatal mucosa may result in the development of an oronasal
fistula. Grafting and appropriate rigid fixation should be
used in palatal expansions of more than 3 mm. Postsur-

Dentofacial Asymmetry
Very few faces are perfectly symmetric. Mild asymmetries of the dental arches and other facial structures are
common and often clinically and functionally insignificant.
Slight asymmetry of the face is likely of little importance
to most patients, but others are very sensitive regarding
facial asymmetry. Therefore, the clinician should warn
patients that very few faces are perfectly symmetric and
that it is impossible to achieve perfect facial symmetry
with treatment.
The many causes of facial asymmetry can be classified
as congenital, developmental, posttraumatic, or pathologic. Some of the more common abnormalities affecting
the face and leading to asymmetry are hemimandibular
elongation, unilateral condylar hyperplasia (hemimandibular hyperplasia), hemifacial microsomia (oculoauriculovertebral sequence), TMJ ankylosis, and deformities
induced by trauma.
Like all orthognathic patients, individuals with facial
asymmetry require a systematic and comprehensive
process of examination, diagnosis, and treatment planning,
as discussed in chapter 2. Because most facial asymmetries
are growth related, special attention should be given to
abnormal growth patterns, prolonged growth, and comple-

212

tion of facial growth. During the clinical examination, the
clinician should pay special attention to three important
factors: (1) the location of the asymmetry, (2) the tissues
involved, and (3) the dimensions involved. The asymmetry
of the face may be in the chin, mandible, maxilla, nose,
orbits, zygoma, frontal areas, or a combination of these
structures. Figure 5-39 presents a way to categorize facial
symmetry patterns. The diagram should also assist the
clinician in differentiating between the specific area and
combination of areas of asymmetry. The involvement of
other facial structures (eg, nose, orbits, and frontal bone)
should be considered, and the soft tissue involvement—
either primary or secondary to the skeletal asymmetry—
should be evaluated.
The clinician should determine what tissues are causing
the asymmetry and whether the involvement is primary
or secondary. This information will influence the treatment plan. For example, soft tissue asymmetry caused by
mandibular asymmetry will be corrected by the skeletal
correction, but the soft tissue deformity seen in hemifacial
microsomia often requires further attention to manage the
deficient or absent soft tissue.
The dimensions involved in the asymmetry should also
be determined. Note that asymmetry of the face seldom
involves only one or two dimensions of space, and correction of the asymmetry in one dimension will certainly
influence the symmetry in other dimensions. The maxillomandibular complex is a 3D structure (Fig 5-40a). To
simplify the determination of the 3D skeletal, dental, and

Dentofacial Asymmetry

a

b

c

Fig 5-40 (a) Diagram of the three dimensions of the maxillomandibular complex. (b and c) Constructing a pyramid representing the maxillomandibular
complex can assist in visualizing the 3D alterations of the midface and lower face during the correction of facial asymmetry.

soft tissue alteration required for the correction of dentofacial asymmetries, a pyramid can be constructed to represent
the maxillomandibular complex (Fig 5-40b). This pyramid
helps the clinician visualize and evaluate the various 3D
surgical movements (Fig 5-40c). Figure 5-41 shows the effect
of unilateral condylar hyperplasia on the spatial position of
the prism. The opposite effect is demonstrated in Fig 5-42.
In situations of facial asymmetry involving three dimensions, the transverse cant of the occlusal plane obviously
differs from anterior to posterior. 3D deformities can be best
assessed by combining the data from the lateral, basal, and
anteroposterior cephalometric radiographs with articulated
dental casts (recorded with a facebow and articulated on
an adjustable articulator) and clinical observations. As in
the correction of other dentofacial deformities, the anteroposterior vertical and transverse positions of the maxillary
incisors play an extremely important role in the final treatment planning (see chapter 2).
Neither presurgical nor postsurgical orthodontic treatment for dentofacial asymmetry differs significantly from
the orthodontic treatment in other dentofacial deformities. However, the clinician should consider the following
guidelines for presurgical orthodontic treatment in cases
with facial asymmetry:
• As in the previously discussed cases of dentofacial
deformities, the dentition should not be orthodontically compensated for skeletal disharmony.
• An existing cant of the occlusal plane should not be
corrected. However, the dental arch should be leveled,
and the orthodontist should ensure that the apical base
midline and incisor midlines follow the cant.

• When skeletal asymmetry exists, the dental midlines
should not be made to coincide but rather be positioned
in the midline of each jaw.
• When facial asymmetry will be corrected by singlejaw surgery, the dental midline of the unoperated jaw
should coincide with the facial midline.
• In patients with hemifacial microsomia and TMJ ankylosis in whom a unilateral open bite is created after
the ramus height is increased, the height should be
maintained while allowing the vertical alveolar growth
of the maxilla.

Asymmetry in the adolescent
The two major causes of severe facial asymmetry in children are hemifacial microsomia and early trauma to the
mandibular condyle. Both conditions primarily affect the
mandible and lead to deficient growth on the affected side.
The maxilla is affected secondarily by the inhibition of
vertical growth of the alveolar process on the affected side.
The basic difference between the two conditions is that in
hemifacial microsomia, missing soft and hard tissue structures may affect the growth potential, whereas in ankylosis,
all structures are present but underdeveloped because of
the lack of function. The basic principle in treating these
conditions is to restore and maximize the expression of
growth so that the development of both hard and soft
tissue structures can be as normal as possible.
Mandibular asymmetry can also develop as a result of
excessive growth on one side of the mandible. The clinician
should differentiate between hemimandibular elongation
and hemimandibular hypertrophy depending on which part
of the mandible is involved. Hemimandibular elongation

213

5 | Diagnosis and Treatment of Complex Dentofacial Deformities

Fig 5-41 Unilateral increase in the height of the

(more posteriorly than anteriorly), and the apex of
the pyramid will rotate forward and to the right.
(a) Frontal view. (b) Lateral view.

a

a

b

b

starts during puberty and is caused by rapid enlargement
of the body of the mandible. The excessive growth is fast,
and the maxilla is not involved; as a result, a unilateral open
bite develops on the affected side and a crossbite develops
on the nonaffected side. Hemimandibular hypertrophy, or
unilateral condylar hyperplasia, is characterized by a diffuse
enlargement of the condyle, condylar neck, ramus, and body
of the mandible. The excessive growth process is slow and
often starts before puberty. Because of the slow growth of the
mandible, the maxilla follows, and an occlusal cant develops.
Surgical correction should involve surgery to both jaws.

The form and function of the TMJ can be affected by
several conditions, such as the following:

Fig 5-42 Unilateral lack of vertical development

Conditions altering the form, size, and height of the
mandibular condyle will inevitably influence TMJ function,
occlusion, and facial esthetics (balance) as well as normal
facial growth in individuals who are still growing. TMJ
reconstruction should therefore form an integral part in
the orthognathic treatment planning for patients requiring
correction of dentofacial deformities.
The TMJ can be reconstructed by using autogenous
bone grafts or alloplastic grafts. For many years, costochondral grafts have been a choice method for reconstructing
the TMJ in pediatric patients; however, sternoclavicular
grafts and grafts harvested from the iliac crest or calvarium
have also been used. Costochondral grafts are favored
because these grafts are easy to harvest, they usually fit
the native TMJ dimensions, and the cartilaginous section
can be adapted to fit the glenoid fossa. Another advantage
is its purported ability to provide for growth, matched to
the remaining facial skeleton (see case 14, Fig 5-52).
Although the growth potential of a costochondral graft
is considered an advantage in growing children, its longterm growth is unpredictable. Unfortunately, complications are common, such as reankylosis in ankylotic patients,
unpredictability of overgrowth, resorption of the grafts, and

Hemifacial microsomia
(oculoauriculovertebral hypoplasia)
Hemifacial microsomia is a progressive asymmetric
craniofacial deformity with a wide range of severity. It
is the second most common congenital facial anomaly (1
in 5,600 births) after cleft lip and palate and involves the
skeletal, soft tissue, and neuromuscular components of
the first and second pharyngeal arches. The phenotype
may vary from slight mandibular asymmetry to cleft lip
and palate, microtia, lateral facial clefts, underdevelopment or absence of the condyle, mandibular ramus, and
zygoma combined with several head and neck and other
congenital abnormalities.
Although several classifications of the severity of
the deformity have been developed that may act as a
treatment guide, treatment should not be based on a
specific classification. The surgical treatment design and
timing of surgical correction for each individual defor-

mity should be carefully planned and based on the individual’s morphologic anomalies, function, growth, and
psychosocial adjustment. According to Posnick (2014),
the basic treatment principles during infancy and early
childhood should be based on airway, feeding, vision,
hearing, and psychosocial support.
Stages
The treatment can be generally divided into eight stages.
First stage (3 months)
The cleft lip is corrected.
Second stage (9 to 14 months)
The cleft palate is corrected.
Third stage (2 to 5 years)
The cranio-orbital asymmetry is corrected.
Fourth stage (5 years or older)
The zygomatic deformity is corrected.

Kaban (1981) has classified the skeletal defects of the
mandibular ramus and TMJ as follows:
• Type I. All the structures are present with normal shapes,
but they are small with a hypoplastic TMJ (Fig 5-43).
• Type II. The mandibular ramus is small and deformed
with a hypoplastic TMJ.
– Type IIA. The hypoplasia of the TMJ is mild and the
position acceptable for symmetric function. The
native TMJ usually does not require replacement
(Fig 5-44).
– Type IIB. The condyle is hypoplastic and so far medially, anteriorly, and inferiorly displaced that a new
joint should be constructed. Normal jaw motion and
function are progressively affected while facial asymmetry is obvious (Fig 5-45).

This section concentrates on the principles of the
orthognathic correction of patients with hemifacial
microsomia based on mandibular defects. The clinician
has several treatment options depending on the age of
the patient and the severity of the mandibular deformity.
Treatment options
1. No treatment until completion of growth followed by
orthognathic surgery.
2. Distraction of the mandibular ramus and body followed
by orthognathic surgery at completion of growth.
3. Costochondral graft followed by orthognathic surgery
(if required).
4. Alloplastic joint replacement followed by orthognathic
surgery later (if required).
5. Alloplastic joint combined with orthognathic surgery
at completion of growth.

Keeping this in mind, the treatment options should be
considered based on the classification.

Treatment options
• No treatment before completion of growth
• Orthognathic surgery (growth completed)

Case 9
A 19-year-old woman presented with Kaban Type I hemifacial microsomia. Apart from the mandibular defects, she

by using a subdermal fat graft (Figs 5-47l to 5-47o). The
treatment outcome is demonstrated in Figs 4-37p to 5-37s.

Case 10
This adolescent girl presented with Kaban Type I hemifacial
microsomia. Her orthodontic treatment started at age 15
years, and her orthognathic surgery was performed at age
17 years. Her facial asymmetry involving hard and soft tissue
is evident in Figs 5-48a to 5-48c. The surgery was performed
in two phases: (1) orthognathic surgery for the correction
of her skeletal deformities, followed by (2) augmentation
220

of her soft tissue deficiency with a subdermal fat graft. The
postsurgical changes are demonstrated in the panoramic,
lateral, and posteroanterior cephalometric radiographs
(Figs 5-48d to 5-48i). Her facial symmetry, profile balance,
and occlusion improved following orthognathic surgery
(Figs 5-48j to 5-48r), but the right mandibular angle defect
caused by the deficient masseter muscle is evident. The final
treatment outcome is demonstrated in Figs 5-48s to 5-48u.

Type IIA
Clinical signs
• Moderate degree of hypoplasia of the glenoid fossa,
condyle, and ramus.

• The masticatory muscles have variable degree of hypoplasia.
• Retrognathia with chin asymmetric to the ipsilateral side.
• Often, a Class II occlusion on the affected side with
asymmetric dental midlines.
• Moderate maxillary occlusal cant.
221

5 | Diagnosis and Treatment of Complex Dentofacial Deformities

Treatment options
• No treatment before completion of growth.
• Distraction of the deficient elements.
• Orthognathic surgery (growth completed).

Type IIB
Clinical signs
• The condyle is severely deficient and medially displaced.
• The masticatory muscles are moderately to severely
deficient.
• The facial asymmetry is excessive.
• Cant in the maxillary occlusal plane.
• Lower dental midline is displaced to the ipsilateral side.
• Posterior crossbites.
• Cases are functionally similar to Type III cases.
Treatment options
• Distraction of the deficient elements. The author is not in
favor of distraction in these patients. The 2D distraction
vector is inaccurate, and the absence of the glenoid
fossa makes docking questionable.
• Reconstruction of the TMJ with a costochondral graft.
Due to the unpredictability regarding growth potential
(graft resorption, no growth, or graft overgrowth), the
indications for TMJ reconstruction by means of costochondral grafts are limited. An additional disadvantage
is the poorly developed glenoid fossa, making stable
positioning of the proximal graft doubtful.
• Distraction of the affected side. This may be indicated
following costochondral grafting and poor growth of
the graft and should be considered overcorrection.
• Reconstruction of the TMJ with a patient-matched graft.
The graft has no growth potential if used in the adolescent patient, so overcorrection should be considered.
When an alloplastic TMJ graft is used in a growing individual, it does not preclude mandibular advancement
by means of a sagittal split osteotomy at a later stage.

This 8-year-old girl was referred by her orthodontist for a

Surgical treatment
Considering the patient’s main complaint and age, the decision was made to perform the TMJ surgery rather than waiting
for further growth. Three surgical options were considered:

1. Reconstruction of the TMJ using a costochondral graft
2. Reconstruction of the TMJ using an alloplastic patientmatched implant
3. Distraction of the malformed condyle

224

The literature and the author’s experience have shown
poor results with condyle distraction, and the inaccuracy
of the vector of distraction of the condyle and the absence
of a glenoid fossa are further disadvantages for this option.

(see page 214), it was decided to use a patient-matched

designed (see Figs 5-49e and 5-49f). The existing condyle
and muscle attachments to the condyle and coronoid
process were maintained. This facilitated symmetric
mouth opening. Vertical and anteroposterior overcorrection was planned with the lower dental midline to the right
and a posterior open bite on the left.
Postsurgical orthodontics
The patient was referred to the orthodontist for orthodontic observation, and preliminary orthodontic correction
started at age 11 years. The 3-year postsurgical results are
demonstrated in Figs 5-49g to 5-49l.

Case 12
This patient first presented with Kaban Type IIB hemifacial microsomia at age 2 years (Fig 5-50a). At the age of

has been completed.

Type III
Clinical signs
• Absence of the glenoid fossa, condyle, and ramus.
• Masticatory muscles have a severe degree of hypoplasia.
• Retrognathia with chin displaced to the ipsilateral side.
• Anterior open bite and posterior crossbites.
• Transverse cant of the maxillary occlusal plane.
• No translation on the affected side.
Treatment options
• Reconstruction of the TMJ with a costochondral graft.
• Reconstruction of the TMJ with a patient-matched
implant.
• Orthognathic surgery can be performed at the same
time as the joint reconstruction. Patient-matched
implants allow for more accurate orthognathic surgery.

Presurgical orthodontics
The initial orthodontic treatment consisted of leveling
and coordination of both dental arches. No attempt was
made to correct the dental midlines or the posterior crossbites. The mandibular incisor midline was placed in the
middle of the mandible. No attempt was made to correct
the bimaxillary protrusion.

228

Surgical treatment
First phase. A 3D virtual treatment plan was designed and
consisted of the following:

• A unilateral sagittal split ramus osteotomy on the right
side of the mandible advancing the mandible, assisting
with the correction of the asymmetry and the correction of the occlusal cant (Fig 5-51m). A prefabricated
intermediate surgical splint was used (see Figs 5-51o
and 5-51q).
• Surgical removal of the “floating” bone in the infratemporal area (Figs 5-51n to 5-51p). The left mandibular TMJ and the mandibular angle were reconstructed
with a patient-matched implant. The patient-matched
implant corrected the mandibular asymmetry and the
occlusal cant and advanced the left side of the mandible and reconstructed the left mandibular angle (Figs
5-51q to 5-51t).
• Superior repositioning of the left maxilla to correct the
cant in the maxillary occlusal plane.
• The chin was advanced and mandibular symmetry
refined by means of a sliding genioplasty.
The postoperative results are demonstrated in Figs
5-51u to 5-51gg.
Second phase
• Augmentation of the left masseter muscle and preauricular area using a free fat graft harvested from the
buttock (Figs 5-51hh to 5-51ll).
Third phase
• Reconstruction of the left ear using rib cartilage.
Postsurgical orthodontics
• Finalizing the occlusion and retention.

There are several advantages to postponing definitive
reconstruction until skeletal maturity:
• Avoid injury to dentition and inferior alveolar nerve.
• Avoid soft tissue scarring.
• Avoid reduction of mandibular marrow space that will
complicate future surgery.
• Minimize negative psychosocial memories of earlier
jaw surgery.
• Avoid iatrogenic 3D deformities that may result in
multiple corrective procedures that will limit longterm results.

Fig 5-51 (cont) (jj to ll) Postoperative frontal, three-quarter, and profile views 7 days after soft tissue augmentation using a subdermal fat graft. Note that

TMJ ankylosis
True ankylosis is caused by either fibrous or bony fusion of
the structures contained within the TMJ capsule. In its most
severe state, it is characterized by a bony union of the condyle
and the glenoid fossa. Mandibular movements are restricted
and, depending on the patient's age, will limit normal growth
on the affected side, causing significant facial asymmetry.
Early surgery that releases the ankylosis followed by active
functional therapy is indicated for these patients. Surgical
release should include removal of all soft and hard tissue
(including the coronoid process) as well as mandible release.
This surgical procedure may be technically difficult, and it
is futile to perform it without physical therapy to maintain
the mouth opening obtained at surgery; either reankylosis
of the joint will occur, or scarred soft tissue will limit mouth
opening. In adult patients with ankylosis, the joint can be
reconstructed by either placing a joint prosthesis or leaving an
adequate gap between the mandible and the glenoid fossa, the
so-called gap arthroplasty. The importance of postoperative
physiotherapy cannot be overstated.
The surgical treatment for young patients with ankylosis can be performed in two stages.
First stage (5 to 7 years old)
The first stage is surgical release of the ankylosis, followed
by long-term physical therapy. A costochondral graft may
be indicated to reconstruct the TMJ and correct the vertical and anteroposterior dimensions of the mandible on the
affected side. This will create an open bite, which will allow
vertical maxillary growth under orthodontic control. The
unpredictable tendency for either resorption of the graft,
excessive growth potential of the costochondral graft, and
the tendency to reankylose should be considered. Due

232

to the low success rate of costochonral grafts, the graft
often requires replacement by means of an alloplastic TMJ
prosthesis (see case 15).
Second stage (14 to 16 years old)
The second surgical stage involves the final correction of
facial asymmetry and occlusal cants with orthognathic
surgery.

Case 15
A 14-year-old boy was referred by his orthodontist for
the treatment of left TMJ ankylosis. His mother gave the
history that the patient developed septicemia at the age of
3 years. The infective process caused septic arthritis of

developed ankyloses of his right TMJ with a maximum
mouth opening of 20 mm, facial asymmetry, and pain
on mastication. At the age of 10 years, the ankylosis was
treated with a costochondral graft, and his mouth opening
improved to 35 mm. His orthodontist manufactured an
appliance that would enhance vertical development of
the maxilla on the affected side. Unfortunately, the costochondral graft reankylosed and overgrew, developing into

surgeon at the age of 3 years; however, he walks with a

mouth opening.

Transverse asymmetry
Transverse asymmetry of the chin is corrected by a sliding
genioplasty that repositions the chin toward the left or
right side (Fig 5-54a).
Cant of the lower border of the chin
A cant of the lower border of the chin is corrected by vertical change at the osteotomy site. The vertical height can
be unilaterally reduced or increased by either downgrafting one side or performing an ostectomy on the other
(Fig 5-54b). An alternative is a propeller osteotomy (see
chapter 6).

Anteroposterior asymmetry
Anteroposterior asymmetry can be corrected by differential anterior or posterior repositioning of the chin (Fig
5-54c). Figure 5-55 demonstrates the correction of asymmetry of the chin in a transverse and vertical dimension.

Asymmetry of the mandible
Most patients with mandibular anteroposterior excess
have some form of asymmetry. Facial asymmetry caused by
mandibular asymmetry can be corrected by surgical repositioning of the mandible. It is important that the orthodontist position the mandibular incisors in the middle of
the chin so that when the dental midlines are corrected,
